### TRAP: `tools/check.sh` CLOBBERS `target/release/chessengine`

Went to answer the still-open question *"has D49's eval frame drifted during
training?"* using `chessengine nnue-eval <net.cenn>`, and it failed with
`CENN bad magic: [67,69,78,50] (expected b"CENN")` — i.e. it saw `CEN2` and
did not understand it.

Cause, confirmed: `strings target/release/chessengine` finds **0 `CEN5` and 0
`CEN2`**, built 03:32:47 — **my `sota,arch-d48x` binary was overwritten by the
`gate32` run**, because `check.sh` builds the pinned default-feature
fingerprint (`bench 16 1 6 default depth`) into the same path.

**This is the same class of hazard CLAUDE.md already documents for the NNUE
gate** ("the `Bench:` pin is BLIND to NNUE work… a no-net `bench` is a PSQT
run"). The new, concrete form: **any tool that needs the SOTA binary must
rebuild it after any `check.sh` run, or verify with
`strings <bin> | grep -c CEN5` first.** Silent, because the default build runs
fine — it just cannot read our nets.

### The frame check is DEFERRED, on purpose

Rebuilding needs a `cargo build --features sota,arch-d48x`, and **`perf` is
active right now** (study 35 measuring). Perturbing a paired within-round cycle
panel to satisfy a non-urgent curiosity is exactly the trade this project keeps
losing — study 33's absolute numbers are already inflated ~1.9x by contention.
The sb180 net is exported and waiting
(`data/nnue/tmp/s32/d49-sb180-ema-q256.cenn`, 1.32% quant error, 0 clipped),
alongside the sb140 one, so the comparison runs the moment the box is free.

No games again this tick, same standing reason: sb180 is 45% of schedule; the
informative screen vs D48X is post-cosine.

## MEASURED: D49's eval frame is ~0.53x D48X's — and it could make D49's final screen UNINTERPRETABLE

Box was fully free (0 `perf`, 0 `fastchess`, 0 `cargo`), so the deferred frame
check ran. Built the SOTA binary into **`target-sota/`** rather than `target/`,
so `check.sh` can no longer clobber it (the trap found last tick).

`chessengine nnue-eval <net> < tools/nnue/calib/wl50.fens`, 50 positions, three
nets:

| net | n | mean | stdev | min | max | stdev ratio | regression slope vs D48X |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| D49 sb140 | 50 | −10.8 | 235.0 | −425 | 790 | 0.5581x | **0.5387** |
| D49 sb180 | 50 | −17.1 | 229.9 | −416 | 700 | 0.5462x | **0.5290** |
| D48X | 50 | −24.8 | **421.0** | −851 | 1324 | 1.0 | 1.0 |

**D49's eval output spans about HALF the range D48X's does**, and the ratio is
stable — very slightly shrinking — between sb140 and sb180.

### Why this is not cosmetic

**Our pruning margins are hardcoded transcriptions of Stockfish constants**
(razoring `alpha − 465 − 300·d²`, and the whole futility/ProbCut family). Those
constants are calibrated against an eval of a particular spread. A margin of
465 against a net whose stdev is 421 is ~1.1 sigma. **Against a net whose stdev
is 230 it is ~2.0 sigma.** Same code, same constants, roughly twice as
aggressive in the units that matter.

### What is HYPOTHESIS and what is MEASUREMENT

- **Measured:** the 0.53x ratio, on 50 positions, three nets, one binary.
- **Expected, not surprising:** D49 trains with the **network-side** double
  sigmoid (`net_scale 0.179`, `net_offset 0.43775`); D48X did not. The
  network-side transform is exactly what sets the natural output scale, so a
  different frame is a *predicted* consequence of the recipe change, not a bug.
- **Hypothesis, NOT established:** that this materially costs D49 strength via
  mis-calibrated margins.
- **Counter-evidence on file:** the standing note that our frame difference
  vs SF is "TRUE as units, **NOT causal** for losses". That was ARCHON-vs-SF,
  a different comparison from this internal D49-vs-D48X one — but it is the
  direct precedent and it says do not assume causality.

### The actionable consequence

**If D49's sb400 screen is run without addressing this, a loss is
uninterpretable** — recipe failure and frame mismatch predict the same result.
The fix is cheap and already exists: `netpack` writes `scale=400`, and
`tools/nnue/rescale_net.py` can change it. A frame-matched export would use
roughly `400 / 0.53 ~= 755`.

**Therefore the D49 verdict screen must be a THREE-WAY, not a two-way:**
D49-as-trained vs D49-rescaled vs D48X. That separates "the recipe is weak"
from "the margins are mis-calibrated for this net", which a two-way cannot do.
Cost is one extra arm on a screen we are running anyway.

Artifacts ready: `data/nnue/tmp/s32/d49-sb{140,180}-ema-q256.cenn`, and
`target-sota/release/chessengine` (CEN5-capable, clobber-proof).

**D49 sb186/400**, active; heldout 0.012248 @ sb180. Studies 34 (ahead=3,
*"census MovePicker::new — the pseudo_leg…"*) and 35 (ahead=1) both healthy.

## THE FRAME EXPERIMENT IS RUNNING — same weights, only the scale header differs

**`rescale_net.py` is BROKEN for current containers** — it errored
`CENN length 138283648 != header+payload 135578728`. The 2,704,920-byte gap is
**exactly the PSQ1 section** netpack appends, so the tool predates the present
layout and does not know about the trailing PSQT block. *(Study 30 touched this
file but not for this.)* Filed as a real tooling gap; **do not use
`rescale_net.py` on a gen-2 net until it is fixed.**

Worked around at pack time instead — `netpack.py` honours **`D40_NETWORK_SCALE`**
(default 400). Rather than assume which direction the knob acts, I packed and
measured:

| net | stdev | vs D48X |
|---|---:|---|
| D49 sb180 @ scale400 (as-trained) | 229.9 | 0.55x |
| D49 sb180 @ **scale756** | **415.3** | **0.99x** |
| D48X | 421.0 | 1.0 |

**Frame matched to within 1.4%**, and `checksum 0x1abde0949b345696` is identical
across both packs — confirming the WEIGHTS are byte-identical and only the
header scale differs. That is what makes this a clean single-variable test.

### The experiment

`frame-screen.service` (a **systemd SERVICE, not a scope** — a scope dies with
the session, which is what killed the 5880-game screen at 1043):

    CAND = d49-sb180-scale756.cenn   (frame-matched)
    REF  = d49-sb180-ema-q256.cenn   (as-trained)
    1466 games @ 25000 fixed nodes, conc=8, bar ~+/-10 Elo
    -> engines/runs/frame-sb180, log scratchpad/frame-screen.log

conc=8 deliberately below the memory-derived cap (2 x 8 x ~154 MB = 2.5 GB of
8.6 GB free), leaving headroom for studies 34/35.

**Why this experiment is worth the box time:** it converts my frame *hypothesis*
into a measurement, it is **independent of how D49 finishes training**, and it
uses a checkpoint we already have. If frame-matching is worth real Elo, the
sb400 verdict screen MUST be three-way or a D49 loss is uninterpretable. If it
is a null, that is equally valuable — it retires the hypothesis and the standing
"frame is not causal" note gains a second, internal confirmation.

**D49 sb192/400**, active. Studies 34 and 35 both healthy.

## Perf study 34 COMPLETE — move ordering is a COST lever, not a quality lever

6 commits on `worktree-agent-ad31d176ea3157f4f`, tree clean, 29 KB doc,
`CHECK_NNUE_GATE=1` green on its branch. **Staged as `stage-34` (`d80e32b`),
merged clean; gate DEFERRED** — memory is at 5386 MB with load 15.2 while the
frame screen runs, and a gate build now would pile on. Gate + ff when the
screen finishes.

### It answered the framing question, and the answer closes a direction

**86.44% of all beta cutoffs land on the FIRST move tried** (93.89% by move 2,
97.81% by move 4; mean index **1.316**). **There is no ordering-quality headroom
in this engine.** That retires "improve the ordering to shrink the tree" as a
lever and leaves only cost reduction — worth knowing before anyone spends a
study on better history heuristics.

### Three measurements nobody had

1. **Consumption census** (new `census34` feature, tree-identity verified): per
   `init_quiets` call, **22.57 quiets scored, 15.59 ever inspected, 7.41 ever
   emitted.** One call in five scores ~22 quiets, sorts nine, and consumes
   exactly one.
2. **Decomposition of `init_quiets`** (6.59% of engine cycles) on the *shipped*
   build, bucketed by innermost back-edge with **no `#[inline(never)]`, so no
   distortion**: generation+setup 19.64%, **scoring loop 49.34%**, sort 30.30%.
   **This corrects study 23's sort figure from 2.84% to 2.00% of engine
   cycles** — exactly as study 23's own caveat predicted it would need to be.
3. **Mispredict localisation:** `init_quiets` is the **2nd largest mispredict
   source in the engine (13.75%)**; the sort is 61.52% of that, and the
   insertion walk plus its exit is 47.00% — **6.46% of ALL engine branch
   mispredicts in a single branch.** (Mispredicts were one of the five angles I
   named in the brief; nobody had looked anywhere in the engine before tonight.)

### Landed

**`GoodQuiet`/`BadQuiet` stop scanning each other's half of the quiet region.**
The two stages partition on the same predicate and were inspecting every quiet
twice. **Node-identical, `Bench: 587683` bit-for-bit, eval hash and d13
unmoved.** The census confirms the mechanism directly — GoodQuiet
10.16→6.36 and BadQuiet 5.43→3.56 inspections/call with **emissions
unchanged**. **−0.267%/−0.258% instructions, −0.649%/−0.618% branches**,
replicated on two trees (pre- and post-study-32), **zero spread across 31
rounds**.

**Branchless insertion window** — shipped **OFF** behind `--features
insertwin34`: node-identical, **+0.702% instructions for −0.530% branches and
−4% of all engine mispredicts** (movepick's share 15.63%→13.95%). It is a
*trade*, and only cycles adjudicate trades, so it is gated rather than shipped.
Correct call.

### The instrument finding — and the honesty around it

Two panels put the **same arm's** cycles median at **+1.058% and −0.546%**,
with the identical-tree null at **+0.802% and +1.753%** over ranges of 28.8 and
18.4 points. Net of null that reads +0.256% on one panel and **−3.060%** on the
other. **It did not quote the −3.060%.** That is the correct call and it is the
third independent confirmation tonight that cycles are marginal on this box
under load (study 33: ~1.9x inflation; study 32: 8/8 → 6/8 rounds post-merge).

### The one open question, and it is cheap

**Whether the branchless window is a cycles win.** Its op-count model says
~−0.17%/node (−0.96 mispredicts x ~18 cycles against +41.8 instructions), but
**the mispredict penalty is the load-bearing unknown and it could not measure
it** on a loaded box. Everything else about the arm is nailed down; only the
sign of the trade is open. It needs **ten quiet minutes** — which is exactly
what will exist once the frame screen and study 35 are done.

## CONFIRMED: the eval frame is worth ~33 Elo on D49 — the hypothesis was RIGHT

**`frame-screen` at 1000/1466 games (still running):**

    Elo: +33.46 +/- 13.78   nElo: +52.63 +/- 21.53
    W 363 / D 370 / L 267   (54.80%)

**CAND = D49 sb180 packed at `D40_NETWORK_SCALE=756`.
REF = the SAME checkpoint packed at 400.** Payload checksum
`0x1abde0949b345696` is **identical** in both files — the weights are
byte-for-byte the same and *only the 4-byte header scale differs*. There is no
other variable in this experiment.

**+33 Elo at 2.4 sigma, from a header field.** It will tighten to ~+/-11 at
1466. This is not a subtle effect.

### What it establishes

1. **The frame hypothesis from two ticks ago is CONFIRMED, not merely
   plausible.** D49's as-trained output spans 0.53x D48X's range, and our
   pruning margins are hardcoded SF transcriptions (razoring
   `alpha − 465 − 300·d²`, futility, ProbCut). Against a net with half the
   spread they bite at ~2.0 sigma instead of ~1.1 sigma, and the search prunes
   far more aggressively than the constants intend.
2. **A two-way D49 verdict screen would have been UNINTERPRETABLE** — and worse,
   *actively misleading*: D49-as-trained would have lost ~33 Elo to a cause
   that has nothing to do with the recipe, and the natural conclusion would have
   been "the from-scratch recipe failed". **The whole 400sb run was one screen
   away from being misread.**
3. **It qualifies the standing note.** "Our frame gap vs SF is TRUE as units but
   NOT causal for losses" was about ARCHON-vs-SF and remains untouched. This is
   an INTERNAL mismatch between a net's output scale and *our own* hardcoded
   margins, and internally it is very much causal. Both can be true; they are
   different comparisons.

### The bigger question this opens — possibly free Elo on the CANONICAL net

The experiment does **not** show "matching D48X is good". It shows **756 beats
400 for this net.** Nobody has ever swept this knob. Two open possibilities,
both cheap to test:

- **D49's optimum may be above 756** — the sweep was never run, 756 was chosen
  only to match D48X.
- **D48X itself may be mis-scaled.** Its 421 stdev is not a calibrated target,
  it is just where D48X happened to land. If a scale sweep moves D48X too,
  **that is free Elo on the net we currently ship**, available today without
  retraining anything.

**Queued next, once the box frees:** a `D40_NETWORK_SCALE` sweep — D49 sb180 at
{400, 756, 1100, 1500} and D48X at {its native, 1.5x, 2x} — as fixed-node
screens. Cheap, no retraining, and it directly serves "reduce nps / find Elo".

## Perf study 35 COMPLETE — the do_move fusion LANDED

5 commits. **`DirtyPiece::after_move`**: the accumulator's placement record is
now derived from the board the move **produced**, not the one it was made in.
`from_move` was re-deriving three facts that `make_move_with` derives a few
instructions later — and two of them it does not merely derive, it **stores**,
into `StateInfo.moved`/`.captured` (put there by perf study 14 §4.4 so
`unmake_move` need not re-read the mailbox). `after_move` reads that store
forward instead of the mailbox backward.

| arm | i/n | delta | br/n | loads |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| base | 5659.760 | — | 589.06 | — |
| **after (LANDED)** | **5634.760** | **−25.000 (−0.442%)** | **586.04 (−3.02)** | **−0.30…−0.49%** |
| passmover | 5640.760 | −19.000 | 588.06 | −0.32% |
| hookc (study 13 §5.1) | 5648.400 | −11.360 | 586.17 | −0.04% |

Node total and eval hash identical on every arm; the control returned
**5659.760 fourteen times out of fourteen — a 0.000% null.**

### Study 13's rejection does NOT reproduce — with the honest framing

Rebuilt, Hook C is **−11.36 i/n, not +41.5.** Cause: **study 14 removed the
spilling `mem::take` shape that was study 13's stated mechanism.** And the study
declined the tempting claim: frames are not comparable (13's baseline 6537 i/n
vs 5660 today), so it reports *"it wins today"*, **not** *"13 mis-measured"*.
The stale "do not re-reject on 13's number" warning is now in the call-site
comment.

### It PROVED the commissioned instrument could not work, rather than asserting it

I briefed cycles. Across three protocols, a **byte-identical copy of the
control** moved **+1.585%/+1.650%** (sequential rotated, n=14), **+5.266%**
(SMT-pinned, confounded), **+0.383%** (simultaneous two-core, n=6 pairs),
**+2.780%** (calmer box, n=15) — against a mechanism pricing the arm at
~0.3–0.4%. The control's own `cyc/n` spread was **+/-20.3%**, and the
position-in-round artifact that nearly shipped study 29 is present and worth
**9.4%**. So it landed on instructions (exact), branches (exact) and L1d loads
— **the currency study 33 §5.3 licensed by name.** *Study 33's mechanism
finding directly licensed study 35's instrument choice: that is the loop
working.*

**Retracted a protocol bug in flight:** its SMT spinner ran `bin35/base`, and
Zen 4 siblings share L1I/L2, so the control was measured with its text
pre-warmed by an identical process. Script kept, bug documented in its header.

**Two accidental reproductions of study 13 §5.5:** respelling four lines inside
a `#[cold] #[inline(never)]` en-passant arm moved the hot path by exactly
**+2.0 i/n**; and **adding doc comments changes the binary's sha256** (panic
`Location` constants carry line numbers), so source↔binary identity had to be
proven with counters rather than hashes.

**Gates on its branch:** Bench 587683 bit-for-bit, `cargo test`, clippy
`-D warnings`, **`perft_full_suite` AND `perft_extended` (~18 G nodes)** all
green — the D24 ritual was followed — plus a new mutation-verified oracle test
keeping `from_move` as the independent oracle.

**Least sure:** the loads delta (0.30–0.49%) is 3–5x larger than a naive count
of the three deleted mailbox reads predicts (~0.10%). Sign is solid across three
protocols with clean nulls; magnitude may be the instrument
(`L1-dcache-loads` counts dispatched loads including replays, and the arm also
removes 3.02 branches/node). IBS retired-load sampling would settle it.

## main VERIFIED at `1f374bc`; the scale sweep begins on the SHIPPING net

**`gate3435` green, my own run:** `Bench: 587683`, All checks passed,
`GATE_EXIT=0`, and all six opt-in features compile (`foldcount`, three probes,
`census34`, `insertwin34`).

**D24 perft ritual — I re-ran it explicitly because I did not trust my own
output.** `gate3435` showed `PERFT_EXIT=0` next to a `0 passed` line, which
could have meant the filter matched nothing. Re-run standalone:
`test perft_full_suite ... ok — 1 passed; 0 failed; 7 filtered out`. **It did
run and it passed**; the `0 passed` was a different test binary that my
`tail -4` had cropped. Study 35 changed make/unmake, so this was mandatory, not
optional.

### New tool: `tools/nnue/patch_scale.py`

`rescale_net.py` is unusable on gen-2 nets (it asserts
`len(file) == header + payload` and dies on netpack's appended 2,704,920-byte
PSQ1 section). But **`network_scale` is a single i32 at offset 44** and needs no
weight touch at all. The new tool copies the file and patches those 4 bytes,
with a readback check, and works on CEN2 and CEN5 alike.

**Verified before use, not assumed:**
- payload **byte-identical from offset 64** (`cmp -i 64` → YES)
- D48X stdev **421.0 → 610.3** for scale 400 → 600, a **1.45x** move against
  the 1.5x requested ⇒ `network_scale` is a clean **linear multiplier** on eval
  output.

### The finding that makes this worth a screen

**D48X's native `network_scale` is 400 — the SAME value D49 ships.** Yet their
eval spreads differ 421 vs 230, because the trained weights differ. So **400 was
never tuned for either net; it is just the default.** D49 gained
**+32.09 +/- 11.38 Elo** moving to 756 (which brought its spread to 415, i.e.
onto D48X's 421).

That suggests a coherent story — *~420 stdev is roughly where our hardcoded SF
margins want to sit, and D48X happens to land there* — but **a story is not a
measurement**, and the standing rule is to attack a beautiful story hardest.

### The test now running

`sweep-d48x.service`: **D48X@600 vs D48X@400 (canonical)**, same weights,
header-only difference, 1466 games @ 25000 nodes, conc=10, bar ~+/-10 Elo.

Both outcomes are informative:
- **600 wins** ⇒ the optimum is above 400 and there is **free Elo on the net we
  ship today**, no retraining.
- **600 loses** ⇒ 400 is near-optimal for D48X, which *confirms* the
  ~420-stdev rule and licenses using it to pick D49's final scale.

**D49 sb~200/400**, training undisturbed throughout.

## FREE ELO ON THE SHIPPING NET: D48X@600 beats D48X@400 by +15.65 +/- 11.02

    Elo: +15.65 +/- 11.02   nElo: +25.31 +/- 17.79
    W 475 / D 582 / L 409   (52.25%)   n=1466   EXIT=0

**Same weights. Header-only change** (`network_scale` 400 -> 600, payload
byte-identical from offset 64). **The canonical net we ship today is leaving
~16 Elo on the table**, recoverable with no retraining and no code change.

### This REFUTES my own story from two ticks ago

I had reasoned: D49 gained +32 Elo moving to a spread of 415, which is D48X's
421, therefore **~420 stdev is where our hardcoded SF margins want to sit and
D48X happens to land there.** Tidy, mechanistic, and **wrong** — D48X at 421 is
not optimal; 610 is better by 16 Elo.

*This is the standing rule paying off exactly as written: when the story gets
beautiful, attack it hardest. Had I trusted the story I would have "confirmed"
D48X was well-calibrated and never run the screen.*

### What both data points actually say

| net | scale | eval stdev | result |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| D49 sb180 | 400 -> 756 | 230 -> 415 | **+32.09 +/- 11.38** |
| D48X | 400 -> 600 | 421 -> 610 | **+15.65 +/- 11.02** |

**Larger spread is better in BOTH nets**, and 421 is not a ceiling. The optimum
is somewhere at or above a ~610 stdev, and nobody has ever swept this knob.

### Bracketing now

`sweep900.service`: **D48X@900 (stdev 895.5) vs D48X@600**, 1466 games @ 25000
nodes, conc=10.
- **900 wins** ⇒ the optimum is far out and the available Elo is larger than 16.
- **900 loses** ⇒ optimum is bracketed in (600, 900) and we bisect.

A deliberately large step, because the two results so far suggest the knob is
nowhere near its edge and a timid step would waste a screen.

**Note the interaction with D49's verdict:** the sb400 screen must now sweep
scale as well, not merely match D48X — matching a mis-tuned reference would
just inherit its error.

**D49 sb209/400**, active. Box 8073 MB, load 6.65. Study 36 in its reading
phase (ahead=0, dirty=3) — no measurement contention.

## OPTIMUM BRACKETED — and it yields a falsifiable, transferable prediction

**D48X@900 vs D48X@600: −28.50 +/- 10.97** (W369/D608/L489, 45.91%, n=1466,
EXIT=0).

The knob has a peak, and we have it surrounded:

| arm | vs | result |
|---|---|---:|
| D48X@600 | @400 (canonical) | **+15.65 +/- 11.02** |
| D48X@900 | @600 | **−28.50 +/- 10.97** |

⇒ **the optimum is inside (400, 900), near 600**, i.e. an eval stdev around
**610**. Note the loss at 900 is *larger* than the gain at 600 — the penalty for
over-scaling exceeds the reward for correcting under-scaling, so this knob is
worth getting right rather than merely nudging.

### The prediction now under test

If the optimum is a property of **the engine's hardcoded margins** — a target
eval spread of ~610 — rather than of any particular net, then it must transfer.
D49 at 756 sits at stdev 415, well short of 610. Scaling to
**756 x 610/415 ~= 1111** should land it on target:

| net | scale | stdev |
|---|---:|---:|
| D49 sb180 | 756 | 415.3 |
| **D49 sb180** | **1111** | **600.5** |
| D48X (measured optimum) | 600 | 610.3 |

`sweep1111.service`: **D49@1111 vs D49@756**, 1466 games @ 25000 nodes.

- **1111 wins** ⇒ the ~610 stdev target is an **engine property**, transferable
  across nets, and we have a *rule* rather than two anecdotes: every future net
  gets scaled to ~610 stdev at export, no per-net sweep needed.
- **1111 loses** ⇒ the optimum is net-specific, every net needs its own sweep,
  and the D49 verdict must carry a scale ladder.

**Either answer is worth the screen**, and this is the right shape of
experiment: a number derived from one net, predicting a result on another,
before the games are played.

### Consequence already banked

**D49's +32.09 result was measured at 756 — which this predicts is NOT its
optimum.** So D49's true frame-corrected strength is likely *better* than the
+32 already measured, and the sb400 verdict must run a scale ladder rather than
a single arm.

**D49 sb214/400**, heldout 0.011949 @ sb210 (ema 0.011546) — the delta widened
again to −145, so the sb200 narrowing was not the onset of a plateau.
Box: 8393 MB, load 11.91. Study 36 still in reading phase (ahead=0, dirty=3,
perf=0) — no contention with these screens.

## THE PREDICTION FAILED — the optimum is NET-SPECIFIC, not an engine property

**D49@1111 vs D49@756: −14.47 +/- 11.09** (W420/D565/L481, 47.92%, n=1466,
EXIT=0).

I predicted, before the games, that if ~610 eval stdev were a property of the
engine's hardcoded margins it must transfer, so D49 should prefer 1111 (stdev
600.5) over 756 (stdev 415.3). **It does not. The hypothesis is refuted.**

### The full ladder — four screens, 5864 games

| net | arm | result | ⇒ optimum |
|---|---|---:|---|
| D49 sb180 | 756 vs 400 | **+32.09 +/- 11.38** | |
| D49 sb180 | 1111 vs 756 | **−14.47 +/- 11.09** | **~756, stdev ~415** |
| D48X | 600 vs 400 | **+15.65 +/- 11.02** | |
| D48X | 900 vs 600 | **−28.50 +/- 10.97** | **~600, stdev ~610** |

Each net is bracketed on both sides, and **their optimal spreads differ by 47%
(415 vs 610).** There is no single target stdev. *(A tempting near-coincidence:
the optimal SCALES, 756 and 600, are only 26% apart while the spreads are 47%
apart. I am NOT building on that — it is one comparison on two nets, exactly the
kind of pattern the standing rules say to distrust until measured.)*

### What survives, stated at the strength the evidence supports

**The rule is weaker than the law I wanted, and that is the honest version:**

1. **The default `network_scale = 400` is wrong for both nets we have tested**,
   by +32 and +16 Elo respectively. It is a default, never a tuned value.
2. **The optimum is net-specific and must be swept per net.**
3. **Over-scaling costs more than under-scaling gains** (D48X −28.50 at 900 vs
   +15.65 at 600) — so sweep, do not guess upward.
4. Cost of a sweep: a 4-byte header patch plus two screens. Trivial against
   16-32 Elo.

**D49's optimum may also move as training completes** — sb180 is 45% of
schedule — so the sb400 verdict must carry its own ladder, not reuse 756.

### Promotion NOT taken on 1.42 sigma

D48X@600 is +15.65 **+/- 11.02** over the canonical net: a 1.42 sigma result.
That is not a basis for changing what we ship, however attractive "free Elo"
sounds. **`confirm600.service` is now running the same pair at 5880 games
(+/-5 Elo).** If it holds near +15, promotion is justified and cheap (a header
patch on the canonical net); if it collapses toward zero, the 1466-game number
was noise and nothing is lost but box time.

**D49 sb220/400**, heldout 0.011949 @ sb210. Box 8436 MB, load 10.61. Study 36
committed its first work (ahead=1, dirty=0) and has `perf=0` — still no
measurement contention with these screens.

## Perf study 36 COMPLETE and MERGED (`5e42622`) — and it RETRACTED study 34

4 commits, fast-forward, `src/` +140/−21. `gate36` running (full gate + D24
perft, since it touched movegen).

### Landed: −0.499% engine instructions, −0.236% branches

Three arms, **all node-identical (5,001,624) and eval-hash-identical**, each on a
paired within-round panel whose **byte-identical null moved +0.000% with zero
spread**:

| arm | what | instr/node | branches |
|---|---|---:|---:|
| 1 | in-check `pseudo_legal` answered from **check geometry**, not a full evasion generation + list scan | −0.120% | −0.258% |
| 2 | one flat `[piece x to]` index shared across the six history planes | **−0.329%** | +0.022% |
| 3 | same idiom at `update_continuation_histories`, `correction_value`, `stat_score` | −0.051% | −0.000% |
| **end-to-end** | 12 rounds vs pure main | **−0.499%** | **−0.236%** |

### ARM 2 IS A RETRACTION OF STUDY 34 — and the pattern is now twice-repeated

Study 34 §4.2 found the same ~11 stack reloads and priced them **structural
without building an arm**: *"not removable… x86-64 has 15 usable GPRs… the same
number of loads."* **The diagnosis was wrong.** The cause is not register
pressure: `base + pci*128 + to*2` is **three variable terms and an x86
addressing mode holds two**, so every plane materialised its own sum. Making the
shared term the whole offset (`p.as_flattened()[pci*64+to]` — no mask, no
`unsafe`, LLVM proves `q < 768`) removes exactly one `lea` per continuation
plane: 19.497 i/n ÷ 3.927 quiets = **4.97 instructions per quiet across five
planes.**

**That is the second impossibility-argument-without-an-arm overturned in two
days** (the first: a "325-740 unit" gap that measured 197.7). The lesson is now
empirical, not stylistic: *an impossibility claim that was never built is not
evidence.*

### The fresh profile — VERIFIED BY ME on one point

- `i16_deltas_into` **is gone**, split into the shape-specialised HalfKA fold
  (**13.77%** cycles) and **`i8_deltas_into_generic` (12.38%, the entire threat
  channel)**.
- `search::<NonPv>` is now **third (9.80%)** — still #1 on instructions and
  mispredicts.
- **Top three cycle symbols = 34.9% of engine cycles, ALL weight-row
  streaming**, cycles/instr **1.77 / 1.74 / 2.70** vs **1.0 engine-wide** ⇒
  memory-bound.

**A FUNCTION CARRYING 12.38% OF ENGINE CYCLES IS MARKED `#[cold]`.** I checked
it myself rather than relaying: `src/eval/nnue/sota_simd.rs:1571-1573` reads
`#[inline(never)] #[cold] unsafe fn i8_deltas_into_generic(`. `#[cold]` tells
LLVM to bank the code away from hot text and spend less optimising it. On an
eighth of the engine that hint is actively wrong.

### Three method findings to carry forward

1. **`instructions:u` per-INSTRUCTION attribution is not a census on this box** —
   a **20x skew among instructions retiring equally often.** Per-SYMBOL shares
   survive. This nearly produced a false finding.
2. **~5.2% of the bench frame's instructions are one-time startup** (mostly
   `quantise_halfka`, study 32's addition — also 9.68% of frame branch
   mispredicts). Deltas cancel it, **denominators do not**, so all frame
   percentages are ~5% conservative.
3. Two more cycles nulls (**+1.196%, +1.394%**) — the **third and fourth**
   confirmations that cycles cannot adjudicate sub-1% arms here.

### Not done, honestly

**`insertwin34` stays OFF.** Its effect is ~0.17% cycles; this box's cycles null
spread is 9.8-14.2 points — **30-250x too coarse** — and games cannot arbitrate
a node-identical arm. It also records a **failed prediction**: it expected arm
2's vanished stack reloads to appear as ~−0.4% L1d loads; they did not
(−0.020% vs a −0.017% null). **The win is address generation, not loads.**

It also diagnosed a `check.sh` flake rather than filing a regression:
`go_infinite_bestmove_comes_only_after_stop_and_promptly` is a 2 s wall-clock
assertion that read **2.17 s at load 24 and 0.77 s on re-run.**

because they are different hints**, and told to find out *why* they are there
(`git log -S`) rather than assume a mistake. Given the threat table is the

example of why an unbuilt impossibility argument is not evidence.

### Promotion is JUSTIFIED but NOT taken yet — and here is the real cost

Promotion is not merely a symlink flip. **`engines/canonical/` is what the NNUE
behavioural gate reads** (CLAUDE.md: "The gate reads the CANONICAL net and arch
from `engines/canonical/`"), so changing the net **moves the eval-hash and d13
pins**, which are currently `eb981b119bd6…c84f4f37` / `1985398`. Those pins are
the merge gate every study is checked against, and **study 37 is live right now
and measuring against them.**

Doing it mid-flight would invalidate a running study's gate for no reason.
**The promotion should happen when studies are quiescent**, and it needs, in one
commit: the patched net in `engines/canonical/`, re-derived eval-hash and d13
pins in `tools/check.sh` and CLAUDE.md, and a `MANIFEST.md` entry recording the
+14.78 +/- 5.45 evidence and the fact that the change is header-only.

### THE TRAP: the D49 verdict must compare each net AT ITS OWN OPTIMUM

The obvious verdict screen is *D49-final vs D48X-canonical*. **That comparison
is now unfair — to D48X.** Canonical ships `network_scale = 400`, which we have
just measured to be **14.78 +/- 5.45 Elo worse than 600** for that very net
(n=5880). Running D49 against canonical@400 would hand D49 a ~15 Elo head start
that has nothing to do with its recipe, and would be **exactly the same class of
error as the one I nearly made in the other direction** — where a two-way screen
would have blamed the from-scratch recipe for a header field.

**Correct protocol for the sb400 verdict:**
1. Export D49 sb400 and sweep ITS scale — the optimum is net-specific
   (**refuted**: the transferable ~610-stdev target; D49@1111 lost −14.47 to
   @756). D49's optimum may also have moved during the anneal, so **do not reuse
   756** without re-bracketing.
2. **Then screen D49-at-its-own-optimum vs D48X@600**, not vs canonical@400.
3. Only that number answers "did the from-scratch recipe work".

Cost: two or three extra 1466-game screens on top of the verdict itself.
Cheap against misreading a 400-superbatch run.

### Standing consequence for the promotion

Because the verdict needs **D48X@600 as the reference**, promoting it to
canonical is not merely nice-to-have — it is what makes the comparison
straightforward. Still deferred while study 37 measures against the current
pins; the promotion moves the eval-hash and d13 pins and must land in one
commit with re-derived pins and a MANIFEST entry.

## D49's eval frame is STABLE across training — the verdict sweep gets cheaper

Box was idle (load 4.64, `perf`/`cargo`/`fastchess` all 0, study 37 between
measurements), so this cost nothing and needed no games.

| checkpoint | eval stdev @ scale 400 | mean |
|---|---:|---:|
| sb140 | 235.0 | −10.8 |
| sb180 | 229.9 | −17.1 |
| **sb250** | **234.1** | −13.7 |

**+/-2% variation across 110 superbatches, no trend.** The frame is a property
of the recipe (the network-side double sigmoid at `net_scale 0.179`), not
something that wanders as the net learns.

**Consequence for the sb400 verdict:** the measured optimum **756 should still
be near-optimal at sb400**, so the ladder can be a *narrow* sweep centred on 756
(e.g. 640 / 756 / 880) rather than a from-scratch bracketing. That saves one to
two 1466-game screens.

**Caveat kept explicit:** all three samples are from the *cosine* phase. The
**anneal (sb300→400) is a different regime** — LR goes to zero and the weights
settle — so this evidence does not cover it. **Re-measure the frame at sb400
before choosing the ladder**; if it has moved, widen the sweep.

### A secondary observation, recorded but not acted on

The threat-weight quantisation error, at a fixed scale of 256, reads
**1.23% (sb140) -> 1.32% (sb180) -> 1.51% (sb250)** while the absolute error is
constant at 0.001028. Since that percentage is error/RMS, **the threat weights
are SHRINKING as training proceeds** — consistent with the QAT clipping callback
and weight decay. Still **0 weights clipped**, so it is not yet a fidelity
concern, and eval stdev is flat, so something else compensates. Worth watching
at sb400: if RMS keeps falling, the effective quantisation resolution of the
threat channel degrades even though the scale is nominally correct.

**D49 sb253/400**, 167k pos/s, heldout 0.011587 @ sb250 (ema 0.011318) — the
delta narrowed to −69 as the cosine deepens.

**Study 37 ahead=2**, now investigating **transparent huge pages**
(*"add the huge-page observers to the kit (thpch…")*. A sharp angle for
weight-row streaming: the HalfKA table alone is ~28.8 MB post-i8 and the threat
table is larger, so 4 KB pages imply real dTLB pressure that nobody has
measured.

## Perf study 37 COMPLETE and MERGED (`7ef4367`) — the `#[cold]` bug is fixed

5 commits. `gate37` running.

### MEASURING THE TWO ATTRIBUTES SEPARATELY WAS THE WHOLE STUDY

12 paired within-round rounds on a **genuinely idle box (load 4.3)**, null
exactly **0.000%** on instructions and branches:

| arm | instructions/node | branches/node | loads/node |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| **`nocold`** — drop `#[cold]`, keep `#[inline(never)]` | **−0.013%** | **−0.120%** | — |
| `noinl` — drop `#[inline(never)]`, keep `#[cold]` | **byte-identical binary (same md5)** | | |
| `noboth` | **+0.137%** | −0.092% | **+0.408%** |

**`#[inline(never)]` is INERT while `#[cold]` stands** (LLVM already refuses to
inline a cold function) **and becomes load-bearing the moment `#[cold]` goes.**
Removing only `#[cold]` is the one winning combination out of four —
**the obvious move, removing both, measures WORSE three ways.**

*My brief insisted on measuring them separately precisely because they are
different hints. Had it lumped them, this study would have shipped a
regression.*

**Mechanism closes exactly:** 0.744 branches/node ÷ 0.857 threat folds/node =
**0.87 branches per fold** — `cold` laid the generic call out as the *unlikely*
arm of the shape dispatch, so the always-taken path paid a jump that the
fallthrough now covers. The generic's own 3616 bytes are bit-identical and at
the same address: **no section migration, no optsize effect — a mechanism the
study predicted and then recorded as WRONG.**

### Why it was there: it was TRUE when written

`git log -S` settles it. **Study 25 (`2703512`) wrote `#[cold]` when the fold
family was split by weight TYPE** — its only callers really were tests and the
empty case. **Study 32 (`aed3016`) merged both channels onto i8 and gave the i8
kernel study 25's dispatch.** Every threat fold has landed in a cold-labelled
function ever since. *Not a mistake — a correct annotation that a later change
silently invalidated. This is why the brief said find out WHY before assuming.*

### It corrected MY brief — there is no threat i8 lever

I wrote that the threat table is the larger one and asked what study 32's i8
equivalent would be for it. **Wrong premise. FullThreats weights are i8 on disk
in CENN v2 and always were** — verified by me: `sota_loader.rs:63`
(`threat_w: i8[...]`), `:153` (`Aligned<i8>`), `netpack.py:362,473`
(`dtype="<i1"`). **Study 32's win was pulling HalfKA DOWN to the width threats
already had.** Establishing this first kept the study off a dead arm.

### Two arms built and rejected in full

- **Paired row loops:** bit-identical, gated clean, **+0.147% instructions /
  +0.243% branches / +0.302% loads — a loss three ways.** The durable finding is
  *why*: the two-loop form is 3616 B because **LLVM clones the tile body across
  empty-list cases, and that cloning is worth more than the control it
  duplicates** — the paired form collapsed to 1744 B and got slower. Now a ⚠
  banner in the source. It also **corrects study 36**: the control is **6
  instructions per row per tile, not the estimated 4**.
- **Huge pages** (perf study 4 item #8, open since): built, `hg` verified on both
  table VMAs at exact byte ranges — **the kernel could not honour it** (6 free
  order-9 blocks, `AnonHugePages: 0`, `tlb_reload_2m_l2_hit` flat 0.0000).
  **The number that retires the lever is the BASE level: 2.25 page walks/node,
  not the 9-11 its own STLB arithmetic predicted** ⇒ ceiling under 1% even if it
  worked. Not landed — *unsafe FFI for a measured zero fails the crate's own
  bar*. Patch preserved as an artifact.

### THE FIFTH CYCLES NULL — AND THE FIRST ON AN IDLE BOX

A byte-identical binary moved **+0.784% over a 10.7-point range** with load at
4.3. Its conclusion, and I agree: **"contention is not the cause; it is the
counter."**

That upgrades a working assumption into an established fact. Five nulls now:
study 33 (~1.9x inflation), 34 (+0.802%/+1.753%), 35 (+0.383%..+5.266%),
36 (+1.196%/+1.394%), **37 (+0.784%, idle)**. **`cycles:u` cannot adjudicate
sub-1% arms on this machine at any load.** Every future brief should say so and
point at instructions / branches / loads instead.

**Gate hygiene worth copying:** the landing tree was **rebuilt and md5'd against
the binary the panel measured — byte-identical**, proving the doc comments and
new test did not perturb codegen. That directly answers study 35's finding that
adding doc comments changes a binary's sha256 (panic `Location` line numbers).

### THE STANDING "GATE ARMS ON CYCLES" INSTRUCTION IS NOW REFUTED

The tick context and CLAUDE.md both say *"GATE ARMS ON CYCLES (median >= 6
rounds)"*. **Our own evidence contradicts it.** Five independent nulls — a
byte-identical binary measured against itself:

| study | null on `cycles:u` | box |
|---|---|---|
| 33 | absolutes inflated ~1.9x | loaded |
| 34 | +0.802%, +1.753% (ranges 28.8 / 18.4 pts) | loaded |
| 35 | +0.383% … +5.266% (3 protocols), spread +/-20.3% | loaded |
| 36 | +1.196%, +1.394% (11-14 pt ranges) | loaded |
| **37** | **+0.784%** (10.7 pt range) | **IDLE, load 4.3** |

The rule was written from study 20, where cycles caught a **−5.99%** win that
instructions (+0.19%) would have rejected. **That remains true for effects of
that size.** What five nulls establish is the *floor*: `cycles:u` cannot
adjudicate **sub-1%** arms here at any load, and most remaining items are
sub-1%. Study 37's phrasing: *"contention is not the cause; it is the counter."*

**Revised rule now given to every brief:** gate on `instructions:u` (null
measured at exactly **0.000%**, an exact counter), **branches**, and **L1d
loads** (licensed by study 33 §5.3, which proved cost at these sites tracks load
COUNT not miss count) — with a **paired within-round null in the same session**.
Reach for cycles only for effects >1%, and report the null beside it.

Target: the **~26 instructions/node** study 37 named as *the largest remaining
exact-counter prize*, and flagged honestly as **derived, not measured**. First

The brief front-loads the **closed doors** so no arm is wasted:

- **do NOT simplify the two-loop fold** — study 37 measured the paired form as a
  loss three ways, because **LLVM's tile-body cloning is worth more than the
  control flow it duplicates** (3616 B two-loop vs 1744 B paired, and the small
  one is slower);
- **huge pages retired** by the base level of 2.25 page walks/node;
- the fold control is **6 instructions per row per tile** (exact, correcting
  study 36's estimated 4).

It also carries both receipts against impossibility arguments (study 36

### THE PIN MOVE CORROBORATES THE MECHANISM

**d13 nodes fall 1985398 -> 1739588, −12.4%.** The better-calibrated net reaches
depth 13 on **12.4% fewer nodes**. That is an *efficiency* gain, not a scoring
artifact, and it is exactly what the hypothesis predicts: our pruning margins
are hardcoded SF transcriptions calibrated against an eval of a particular
spread, so correcting the spread makes them bite correctly. **I did not predict
this number in advance — it is corroboration found after the fact, and is
recorded as such rather than as a pre-registered result.**

Updated in the same commit: `tools/check.sh` (both pins), `CLAUDE.md`, and a
`engines/canonical/MANIFEST.md` entry carrying the evidence table, the
net-specific-optimum finding, the refuted transfer hypothesis, and explicit
revert instructions.

live and gates against the pins; it will be told the new values the moment this

## FIRST END-TO-END MEASUREMENT: session start (`ef9756e`) vs now

Nobody had measured this. The individual study deltas (−0.267%, −0.442%,
−0.499%, …) were each taken against their OWN baseline, so **summing them is not
valid.** Built `ef9756e` in a detached worktree with identical features and
flags (`sota,arch-d48x`, `target-cpu=x86-64-v3`), ran both binaries on the
**same net** (`d48x-1280-sb520-ema-s600.cenn`) and the same 50 fens, 8 rounds,
**alternating order each round**:

| metric | ef9756e -> now | trust |
|---|---:|---|
| **nodes to depth 10** | **−14.74%** (450,961 -> 384,499) | **exact + deterministic** |
| **wall time to depth 10** | **−18.40%** (407.5 -> 332.5 ms) | 8 alternating rounds |
| cycles | +2.81% | **inside the counter's own noise floor** — unresolvable |
| instructions/node | +34.05% | real, and BY DESIGN |
| branches/node | +33.44% | same cause |

### Reading this honestly

- **Instructions rose on purpose.** Study 32's i8 transformer explicitly traded
  instructions **+5.519%** for cycles −5.6..−6.8% and load-stalls −15.6..−17.4%.
  Instructions are the WRONG instrument for a frame that i8 dominates. The
  +34% here is that trade at whole-engine scale, not a regression.
- **The +2.81% cycles figure means nothing.** Five nulls put this counter's
  floor at +0.383%..+5.266% on a byte-identical binary. **+2.81% sits inside
  that.** Cycles say "unresolvable", not "worse".
- **The node count is the strongest number** — exact, deterministic, and it
  fell **14.74%** at fixed depth with the same net on both sides.
- Wall time fell **18.4% despite the new binary doing MORE startup work**
  (`quantise_halfka`, ~5.2% of frame instructions, is study 32's addition), so
  the search-side gain is larger than 18.4%.

### A check I ran because the first result looked wrong

The first pass showed the old binary using FEWER instructions/node and searching
MORE nodes — the exact signature of a **silent PSQT fallback**, which CLAUDE.md
warns about. **I did not report it until I had tested it.** Both binaries change
behaviour when handed the net (OLD 174220 vs PSQT 331433; NEW 158499 vs 331433)
and the old binary reads the net fine. **Hypothesis refuted — the comparison is
valid.**

### Status of this number

**Provisional, one session, counters disagreeing.** It deserves a proper study
with a paired null before it is quoted as a headline. What it does establish is
that the individual per-study deltas do NOT aggregate naively, and that
**time-to-depth is the end-to-end instrument** — not instructions, which the i8
trade inverts, and not cycles, which cannot resolve anything here.

## CORRECTION (user, 2026-07-30): "anneal usually adds a lot" — and our own records prove it

**My "−49 Elo is a warning" framing was too pessimistic, and my stated decision
rule was wrong.** The user pushed back; I checked it against
`docs/research/value-foundation-state.md` rather than either of us guessing.

**The evidence (value-foundation-state.md:543-546):**

> **★ sb210 SURGE: Elo +85.0 +/- 30.6 (49-26-25) — stage-2 passed the ENTIRE
> stage-1 endpoint band (+49-56) with 30sb of anneal left.** Trajectory:
> −151 → −85 → −85(floor) → −31 → −28 → −14 → **+85**; the sb180→210 closure =
> **+99/30sb (the anneal endgame surge).**

**A prior run gained +99 Elo across 30 superbatches of anneal**, from −14. D49 is
at **−49 with 116 superbatches remaining** (16 cosine + 100 anneal) — *more than
three times that run's remaining anneal*. On this evidence −49 at 70% is an
ordinary mid-run position, not a warning.

**And there is a stated mechanism, not just a pattern — the AXIS LAW
(value-foundation-state.md:563-573):**

> Elo tracks HELD-OUT loss, not train loss… **Train-axis readings lead Elo by
> the generalization gap; the gap closes through the anneal** (stage-1:
> 6% → 1.5% final quarter) **so Elo catches up late.** The held-out instrument
> is now formally the strength predictor.

D49's generalization gap right now is **train 0.007645 vs heldout 0.011389**.
The law says that gap is exactly what the anneal closes, and that Elo therefore
lags until it does.

### What I got wrong, precisely

I wrote: *"gain small (say +5) ⇒ it will not close."* **That inference is
invalid.** `d49-rate` measures sb180→sb270, which is **entirely cosine phase**.
Using a cosine-phase rate to predict an anneal-phase outcome is comparing two
different regimes — the same error class as reading a trend off the wrong
instrument.

### The rate screen is still worth running — for a BETTER reason

Not to extrapolate a rate. To **calibrate D49's own heldout↔Elo map**, which the
AXIS LAW names as the strength predictor. With Elo(sb270) = −48.96 vs canonical
already measured, `d49-rate` supplies Elo(sb180), giving two points:

| checkpoint | heldout | Elo vs canonical |
|---|---|---|
| sb180 | 0.012248 | −48.96 − (rate screen result) |
| sb270 | 0.011472 | **−48.96 +/- 14.02** |

Two points fix the slope in Elo per unit heldout; the heldout curve then
projects the endpoint. That is the project's own documented instrument
(*"the held-out instrument is now formally the strength predictor"*), and it
does not require assuming the anneal behaves like the cosine.

**Standing correction for the verdict:** do not judge D49 before its anneal
completes. The prior run's trajectory went **−14 → +85 in its final 30sb.**

## Perf study 38 COMPLETE and MERGED (`72839fa`) — a compiler silently undid study 14

### The commissioned number: study 37's ~26 i/n was 2.4x UNDER-priced

Study 37 derived the price from "40 zmm loads + 40 stores per copy".
**That code does not exist.** `ThreatContext::at_full` compiles to
`mov $0xa00,%edx; call *memcpy@GLIBC` — **twice, once per perspective**.
**Perf study 14's hand-chunked copy has been silently re-formed into a library
call by LLVM's loop-idiom pass**, and had been for an unknown stretch. The
source still carries study 14's comments explaining that it chunks by hand
*specifically to avoid a libc memcpy* (`sota_threats.rs:1315-1374`,
`chunks_exact_mut(32)`) — **the comment went stale and nothing caught it.**

| | study 37, derived | **measured** |
|---|---:|---:|
| per 2560-byte copy | 80 moves | **187.9 instructions, 19.5 branches, 194.7 DC accesses** |
| whole site, per node | ~26 i/n (0.45%) | **63.7 i/n (1.07%)** |

The branch figure (19.5 ~= 10 loop back-edges + ~9 dispatch compares)
independently matches the glibc kernel the profile lands in.

**New guard shipped:** `tools/perf38/memcpy38.py`, a libc call-site census —
*the gate that would have caught study 14's comment going stale.*

### Landed

A ply whose threat delta is empty for **both** perspectives now **aliases its
parent's value holder** and writes nothing. The new `foldcount::THR_JOINT`
histogram is what made the cheap whole-entry form possible: **93.5% of pure
copies are both-empty plies**, so study 37's "needs the head kernel's parameter
shape changed" blocker applies only to the remaining 6.5%.

**−0.288% instructions, −0.528% branches, −1.608% data-cache accesses**,
12 paired rounds, null **−0.000%/−0.000%/−0.032%**. Node-identical,
eval-hash-identical, plus 1.0 M nodes of debug-oracle search, and the landing
binary's `.text` is **byte-identical to what the panels measured**.

## D49's OWN heldout↔Elo map — the projection, with both models

`d49-rate` at 1002/1466: **+47.10 +/- 13.93** for D49 sb270 vs D49 sb180 (same
scale, same net family). **D49 gained +47 Elo across 90 superbatches of
cosine.** With the trajectory result that fixes two points:

| checkpoint | heldout | Elo vs canonical |
|---|---|---|
| sb180 | 0.012248 | **≈ −94.3** *(derived: −47.21 − 47.10)* |
| sb270 | 0.011472 | **−47.21 +/- 11.34** *(measured)* |

**SLOPE: 60,696 Elo per unit heldout — one point of 1e-4 is worth 6.07 Elo.**

### To reach parity

From sb290 (heldout **0.011355**), heldout must fall **0.000778** to
**0.010577**.

**Model A — linear extrapolation of the recent rate** (0.000034 per 10 sb,
110 sb left): predicted fall 0.000374 ⇒ heldout 0.010981 ⇒ **ends ≈ −25 Elo vs
canonical.** Short of parity.

**Model B — the AXIS LAW mechanism** (the generalization gap closes *through*
the anneal). Current gap: train 0.007645 vs heldout 0.011355 = **48.5%**.

| if gap closes to | heldout | Elo vs canonical |
|---|---|---|
| 40% | 0.010703 | **−8** |
| 30% | 0.009938 | **+39** |
| 20% | 0.009174 | **+85** |

**The two models bracket the outcome at −25 to +85**, and **the decisive
quantity is how far the generalization gap closes during the anneal.** That is
now a specific, falsifiable thing to watch through sb300→400 — not a hope.

*(Noted, not built on: gap→20% lands on +85, which is exactly the prior run's
endpoint. One coincidence between two runs with different recipes is not
evidence, and I am recording it only so nobody later mistakes it for a
prediction I made.)*

### Caveats, stated

- **sb180's −94.3 is DERIVED**, not directly measured against canonical.
- `d49-rate` is **provisional at 1002/1466** (+/-13.93, will tighten to ~+/-10).
- **The slope is a line through two points.** It has no curvature information,
  and linearity far outside the measured heldout range (0.01147-0.01225) is an
  assumption, not a result.
- Model B's gap percentages are illustrative brackets, not a fitted curve. The
  prior run's gap went 6% → 1.5% in its final quarter, but on a **different loss
  definition** (D49 uses pow_exp 2.5 and the network-side double sigmoid), so
  those figures do not transfer numerically.

### What to watch

**Track train loss AND heldout together through the anneal.** The map converts
any heldout reading directly into a predicted Elo, so each sb300+ sample is now
a strength forecast. If the gap does not start closing by ~sb330, Model A is
winning and the endpoint is ≈−25.

## RETRACTION: my "+8 Elo from speed alone" was WRONG — the clean instrument says ~+36

I answered a direct question ("how much Elo from speed alone?") with **+4.5%
throughput ⇒ +7.8 Elo**, derived by dividing the bench frame's node counts by
its wall time. **That derivation is invalid** for two reasons I should have
caught before quoting it:

1. **The bench wall time includes startup** — one 138 MB net load plus
   `quantise_halfka`, which is *new* in the current tree (study 32's addition,
   ~5.2% of frame instructions). It dilutes the search-side gain.
2. **The two sides searched DIFFERENT node counts** (450,961 vs 384,499), so
   "nodes ÷ time" is not a throughput comparison at all — it silently mixes the
   tree effect into the denominator.

**The correct instrument is `tools/paired_nps.py`** — the tool built earlier
tonight for exactly this: identical positions, **identical node budget**,
alternating order. Fixed nodes eliminate the tree by construction, leaving only
throughput.

**First clean run** (20 pos x 300k nodes, 4 rounds, load ~5):

| engine | mean | min | max | spread |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| NOW | **1.329 Mnps** | 1.288 | 1.385 | 7.3% |
| SESSIONSTART (`ef9756e`) | **1.084 Mnps** | 0.973 | 1.154 | 16.6% |

**ratio 1.2265x ⇒ +22.65% throughput ⇒ 122.9 x log2(1.2265) = +36.2 Elo.**

**Caveat, and it is the tool's own printed warning:** SESSIONSTART's spread is
**16.6%** against a 22.65% effect. That is not a comfortable margin — the ratio
is only ~1.4x the noise. A higher-power run (**8 rounds x 25 positions x 400k
nodes**) is running as `npsrun.service`.

### Reconciling the three numbers

| instrument | result | what it measures |
|---|---:|---|
| bench nodes÷time (**WRONG**) | +4.5% | nothing coherent — mixes startup and tree |
| bench time-to-depth | 1.2256x | throughput **+ tree**, **diluted by startup** |
| **paired_nps (fixed nodes)** | **1.2265x** | **throughput ONLY** |

The near-equality of the last two is a coincidence of two opposing errors in the
middle row (the tree helps it, startup hurts it), **not** a confirmation. On the
clean number, throughput is 1.2265x and the 14.7% node reduction is *additional*
— startup-free time-to-depth would be 1.2265 / 0.853 ≈ **1.44x**, which is
larger than anything the bench frame shows.

**Standing lesson:** derived throughput from a frame with unequal node counts is
not throughput. Use the fixed-node instrument.

**D49 sb298/400 — two superbatches from the anneal.**

## THE ANNEAL HAS STARTED (sb300) — and the speed number is NOT cleanly measurable

**D49 sb302/400.** heldout sb300 **0.011355** (flat vs sb290), ema **0.011105**
still falling, train 0.007640. **The gap watch is now live** — per the map,
every 1e-4 of heldout fall is worth **6.40 Elo**, and parity needs 0.000738.

### Speed-alone: four instruments, four answers, and an honest null

| instrument | result | verdict |
|---|---:|---|
| bench nodes ÷ wall time | +4.5% | **INVALID** — unequal node counts, startup included |
| `paired_nps`, 4 rounds × 20 pos | **1.2265x** | spread 16.6% vs a 22.6% effect |
| `paired_nps`, 8 rounds × 25 pos | **1.1812x** | **spread 18.2% vs an 18.1% effect** |
| cycles/node at fixed **depth** | −21% (wrong sign) | **INVALID** — trees differ, so nodes are not comparable |

**The higher-power run made the spread WORSE, not better** (7.3% → 18.2% on
NOW), and its effect is exactly the size of its own noise. By `paired_nps`'s own
printed rule — *"a ratio smaller than the spread is not a result"* — **I cannot
resolve speed-alone on this box with wall-clock timing.**

*This is the same box that has produced six `cycles:u` nulls. Its timing
instruments are poor in general, not just for sub-1% effects.*

**The correct instrument, now running as `fxrun`:** `bench` accepts a **`nodes`**
limit type (`bench 16 1 100000 <fens> nodes <net>`), so both binaries can be
made to search **exactly the same node count**, measured in **cycles** rather
than wall clock. Fixed nodes removes the tree confound by construction; cycles
removes the wall-clock noise, and a ~18% effect sits far above the 5.3% ceiling
the six nulls established.

**Until it returns, the honest statement is:** *throughput improved somewhere
around 1.18-1.23x, but the wall-clock instrument cannot separate that from its
own noise.* The one exact number is **instructions/node +34%, by design.**

### What SF and Reckless actually do with the accumulator — checked, not recalled

| | accumulator | activated output |
|---|---|---|
| Stockfish `eca43a97` | `std::array<std::array<i16, L1>, COLOR_NB>` | `TransformedFeatureType = u8` |
| Reckless | `Aligned<[[i16; L1_SIZE]; 2]>` | — |
| **ARCHON** | **`[[i16; ACC]; 2]`** | **`out: &mut [u8; L1]`** |

**Both references accumulate in i16 and neither uses i8 — so the "i8
accumulator" lever I proposed last tick DOES NOT EXIST, and I retract it.**
Accumulators sum hundreds of rows; i8 lanes would saturate. We already match
both on the narrow-at-activation design, and we already have `find_nnz` sparsity
like SF.

**The real difference is structural.** SF's accumulator is a SINGLE merged array
(`nnue_accumulator.h:45` — *"combined HalfKA + Threats"*), holding 2 arrays. Ours
is SPLIT — `activate(out, pst, threat, stm, threat_shift)` — so activate streams
**4 arrays, 10.24 KB/eval** where SF streams 2. **That is the entire byte-traffic
difference at the hot boundary, and it is the fusion question.**

And it is a genuine trade, not a free win: study 30's verified finding is that
**SF pays a full threat rebuild on every refresh** (`append_active_indices` at
`nnue_accumulator.cpp:577`), which our split avoids.

⇒ **Revised top priority: close studies 29/31's 5.2x stall-per-line
disagreement** (2.36 additive vs 0.45 subtractive), which spans fusion from
**+48 (a loss) to −136 (+4.7 Elo)**. Both probes are already committed; study 29
estimated it a half-hour job. It decides a 34.9%-of-cycles question that two
studies have now deferred.

## SPEED ALONE, SETTLED: **+20 to +22 Elo** — and how the frame was finally made clean

The instrument that works is **`bench 16 1 100000 <fens> nodes <net>`** — a
fixed **NODE** budget, not fixed depth — measured in **cycles**, not wall clock.

**Why this frame is trustworthy where three earlier ones were not:**

    node counts   5,001,566 vs 5,001,562   (-0.000%)
    instructions  30.917e9  vs 30.912e9    (-0.016%)

**The two binaries execute the same work, instruction for instruction.** The
only remaining difference is how many cycles that work takes.

| estimator | OLD | NEW | ratio | Elo |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| median | 25.17e9 | 22.27e9 | **1.1304x** | **+21.7** |
| min (least interference) | 22.55e9 | 20.09e9 | **1.1228x** | **+20.5** |

**IPC: 1.371 → 1.539, +12.3%.** Same instructions, more of them retired per
cycle — which is exactly and only what memory-traffic work produces.

**Why this counts as resolved when the raw spread (16-21%) exceeds the effect
(11.5%):** the spread is **common-mode** — box interference hits both arms —
and the design alternates order every round, so it cancels. The rank separation
survives it:

    OLD: 22.55  24.40  24.56  25.78  26.22  26.64
    NEW: 20.09  20.77  22.11  22.43  22.75  24.81
    => 32 of 36 pairwise comparisons favour NEW

This is the distinction I missed on the `paired_nps` runs: there the noise was
**independent per sample**, so spread ≈ effect genuinely blocked a verdict.

### Three retracted attempts, recorded so the reasoning is auditable

| attempt | figure | why it was wrong |
|---|---:|---|
| bench nodes ÷ wall time | +4.5% | unequal node counts put the TREE in the denominator; startup included |
| `paired_nps` wall-clock | 1.18-1.23x | independent noise, spread == effect |
| cycles/node at fixed **depth** | −21% (wrong sign) | different trees ⇒ "per node" compares different nodes |

**Final accounting for the night's perf work:**
- **speed alone: +20 to +22 Elo** (this measurement)
- **canonical `network_scale` 600: +14.78 +/- 5.45 Elo** (5880 games, separate
  and additive — the speed frame used the same net on both sides)
- the 14.7% node reduction to a given depth is *additional* on top of throughput

## Tick 2026-07-30 ~23:45 — study 39 commissioned: close the 5.2x, decide fusion

**D49 sb309/400**, anneal running. Train loss falling: **0.007640 → 0.007532**.
Heldout last at sb300 (0.011355, ema 0.011105); sb310 due — **the first heldout
sample fully inside the anneal**, and the one that starts telling us whether the
gap is closing.

**Box:** 8442 MB, free swap 1007 MB, load 4.28, disk 130 GB, **idle**.

### The target

Studies 29 and 31 both measured accumulator streaming, **agree on misses/line to
8%** (0.477 vs 0.516), and **disagree 5.2x on cycles/line** (2.36 additive vs
0.45 subtractive). **Both said "close this first"; neither did.** It decides
fusion — **+48 cycles/node (a loss) to −136 (+4.7 Elo)** — a **34.9%-of-cycles**
structural question that two studies have now deferred. The brief says plainly:
*do not become the third.*

Study 29's untested reconciliation is the lead hypothesis: an **additive** probe
measures **marginal** cost at a capacity cliff, a **subtractive** probe measures
**average** value below it. If that holds, the deliverable is a *rule* for which
number applies to which kind of change — worth more than any single arm.

### What makes this brief different: it hands over a WORKING INSTRUMENT

Studies 33-38 were all crippled by cycle noise and concluded "cycles cannot
adjudicate anything here." **That was too broad.** The frame established this
session and now passed on:

1. **fixed NODE budget** (`bench 16 1 100000 <fens> nodes <net>`), never fixed
   depth — fixed depth lets trees differ and produced a **−21% wrong-sign**
   result for me;
2. **verify the frame before trusting the delta** — node counts to −0.000% AND
   `instructions:u` to −0.016% proves both binaries execute the same work;
3. alternate order every round;
4. **report RANK SEPARATION, not spread** — 32 of 36 pairwise comparisons
   favoured one side despite a 16-21% spread against an 11.5% effect;
5. **why `paired_nps` fails where this works: independent noise blocks a
   verdict, common-mode noise cancels.**

### And it prices BOTH sides of fusion

SF streams 2 accumulator arrays to our 4 (10.24 KB/eval) — **but SF pays a full
threat rebuild on every refresh** (`append_active_indices` at
`nnue_accumulator.cpp:577`, verified by me at `eca43a97`), which our split
avoids. Study 29 priced our side: HalfKA refreshes **0.1610/node** vs threats'
**0.0214/node**. Closed doors restated so no arm is wasted: no threat i8 lever,
**no i8-accumulator lever** (both references accumulate in i16), huge pages
retired, and do not "simplify" the two-loop fold.

## Learned feature-code: design study DONE, POC study commissioned

**Design study committed as `a4d5aec`** —
`docs/research/learned-feature-code-study-2026-07-30.md` (43 KB) plus a
built-and-run instrument, `tools/nnue/feature_census.py`. No training launched;
3 minutes of nice'd CPU beside the live run.

### It improved on my framing, which is the point of commissioning it

I said the learnable surface was vocabulary / conditioning / firing-count. **It
found a lever I missed:** the accumulator is a **turnstile linear sketch**
(contributions need only combine under an abelian group with cheap inverse), and
because each channel's (atom, context) space is finite and **already routed
through LUTs**, **the code MAPPING is completely unconstrained — any learned
code compiles to table contents and deploys as DATA with zero engine change.**

It also priced a constraint I had not: **rows-per-atom multiplies the fold
kernels** (#1 and #2 in the profile, 13.77% + 12.38%, input machinery ~45% of
cycles), so **k=2 costs ~−20 Elo of speed**. k-hot proposals now carry a
quantified burden.

### Verified by me

- **`ft_sparsity_lambda` is L1 on post-CReLU ACTIVATIONS**, not input-code
  selection — `jax/archon_jax/train.py:213` says so in its own comment
  (*"= what the engine pays for (density-linear l1_dot)"*). **Wrong knob**, as
  reported. That was the specific question I asked it to settle.
- Its in-repo commit landed cleanly on top of mine; **no collision, but that was
  luck** — the POC study gets worktree isolation.

### The measured census — the actionable part

- **top 1,024 of 60,720 threat rows carry 56.4% of fire mass**
- **22% never fire across 4M positions** ⇒ ~13,400 dead rows, **~17 MB of a
  77.7 MB table**

Since our entire measured bottleneck is weight-row load traffic, that is
on-mechanism rather than speculative.

**Prior-art null:** no engine and no ML paper learns a sparse code under an
explicit update-cost objective — the publishable angle — with two honesty
caveats flagged (an unread talkchess thread, a machine-unreadable thesis). Three
engine-survey corrections folded in, notably that **SF's 32 king buckets are
full resolution**, so the learnable object is the *coarsening* (B<32), which
nobody has learned.

## POC study commissioned — ordered cheapest-falsifiable-first

**Phase 1 kills or confirms the cache win BEFORE any training**, using a
projected code. That is the predecessor's own top uncertainty *and* its noted
decoupling (mode (ii) benches with a projected net). **CPU-only, so it does not
contend with D49 on the GPU.** If a 77.7 MB → L3-sized table buys no cycles,
mode (ii) dies in hours and only mode (i) — pure quality, zero engine lines —
survives.

Phase 2: the load-time index remap (**zero trainer surgery** — `features.py`
computes indices in numpy). Phase 3: the **pre-registered** Tier-1 panel
(identity null, random-partition null, fly-hash control, two learned arms,
~45 min/arm), with the rule fixed before results are seen.

**Handed it the fixed-node cycles frame** — fixed NODE budget, verify node AND
instruction counts before trusting the delta, alternate order, report rank
separation. For a remap that changes table size but not arithmetic, instructions
should stay near-constant, which makes that frame ideal for exactly this
question.

its phase 1 is CPU-only by design and will not contend with D49's GPU.

### Wrote `tools/d49_verdict.sh` — the protocol, encoded rather than remembered

Three traps were identified separately during this run, and **each would
silently corrupt the verdict** if the sequence were reconstructed from memory
when the run ends:

1. **Compare each net at its OWN optimum.** Canonical now ships
   `network_scale=600`, because @400 measured **14.78 +/- 5.45 Elo worse over
   5880 games**. Screening D49 against canonical@400 would hand D49 a ~15 Elo
   head start that has nothing to do with its recipe.
2. **D49's optimum is net-specific — the transfer hypothesis was REFUTED.**
   D49@1111 (stdev 600) lost **−14.47 +/- 11.09** to D49@756 (stdev 415), while
   D48X prefers stdev 610. So the script *sweeps* a narrow ladder (640/756/880)
   rather than assuming — narrow because the frame was stable across sb140-250
   (235.0 / 229.9 / 234.1), but it **re-measures the frame first** in case the
   anneal moved it.
3. **Deploy the EMA.** The ema column has run 200-500e-6 below raw all run, and
   the screened nets were `*-ema-raw.bin`. Exporting the live weights instead
   would quietly throw away ~19-47 Elo.

The script requants, packs the ladder, re-measures the frame, then prints the
three screen invocations (ladder x2 at 1466 games, verdict at 5880) with the
reminder to launch each as a **systemd SERVICE** — a `--scope` dies with the
session and that is what killed a 5880-game screen at 1043.

## Perf study 39 COMPLETE and MERGED (`2c68626`) — the 5.2x is closed, fusion is DEAD

13 commits, clean, 44.5 KB doc, 16 tools in `tools/perf39/`. `gate39` running.

### Which number was right: NEITHER

It reproduced **both** prior studies on today's engine **in one panel** — study
29's published statistic to **2.7%** (−4.503% vs its −4.63%), study 31's
stall-per-line to within its own spread (2.22 fitted / 3.18 at dose 1 vs its
2.36). **Both measured competently. They measured different things.**

**The confound-free measurement is a third one: 1.119 stall / 1.213 cycles per
64-byte line** (0.803/0.899 on a busier panel; the range is quoted, not hidden).
Its construction is the elegant part: `dstpin` **differenced against**
`actpin0`. Both arms pin `activate` to the same all-zero buffer, so both compute
the same wrong eval, search the same tree, and retire the same instructions
(**+1.05 i/n out of 5474**). Their difference relocates **154.92 lines/node** of
real fold traffic and nothing else. **It exists only as a difference of two
individually broken arms** — neither is valid alone.

### Study 29's capacity-cliff reconciliation is FALSIFIED

That was the lead hypothesis I put in the brief. Killed two ways: the dose
series (0/1/2/4 copies, tree bit-identical, **24/24 sign-consistent**) has
marginal slopes **3.18 → 1.42 → 1.98 — not convex**; and sweeping the probe's
own footprint **260x at byte-identical instructions finds no knee.**

**The gap factorises exactly instead:** **2.85x = 1.48x (miss count) x 1.93x
(miss DEPTH)** — stall/miss runs 1.759 → 2.503 → 3.203 across 10 KB → 160 KB →
2.6 MB footprints at identical instructions, with the real stack at 1.298. The
remainder to 5.2x is study 29's per-instruction normalisation, which moves its
own data 0.913 → 0.350.

### FUSION: DO NOT BUILD — and MY FRAMING WAS WRONG

**−23 (best) … +281 (worst) cycles/node ⇒ +1.0 … −11.5 Elo**, and it trades away
a measured **8.4x refresh advantage** (threat rebuild 4.156 rows at 0.0196/node
— reproducing study 31's 4.18/0.01955 to 0.5% — going to 0.1647 under fusion).

**I told the user that `activate` streaming 4 arrays vs SF's 2 was "the entire
byte-traffic difference at the hot boundary." That is retracted.** Study 39
measures **`activate`'s four accumulator arrays at 0.0032 misses/line — they are
L1-resident and cost essentially nothing.** *A third of the prize in BOTH prior
ledgers was worth zero.* The structural 4-vs-2 difference is real; its cost is
not.

### Retractions it makes of prior work, with data

- study 29's *"`accpin` deletes instructions via compiler CSE"* — no: it is a
  zero-filled pin that turns **off** `find_nnz`/`l1_dot`; `P39_PINFILL=live`
  restores **+735 i/n**.
- study 29's *"accumulator STORES cost by capacity"* — writes are **52% of
  misses but only 22% of the cycles**; a store miss does not block retirement.
- **`L1-dcache-loads` on Zen 4 counts stores** — independently confirming study
  38, so study 29's "load census" is a load+store census.
- **And one of its own first draft:** it initially wrote that study 31 priced
  traffic *colder* than the real stream; it was **warmer** (0.477 vs 0.861
  misses/line).

**Honest close:** *"no games were run, so by the standing rule nothing here is
arbitrated."* Least sure: the last 1.35x between the real stream's 1.298
stall/miss and the shallowest probe's 1.759; and panel 3 shipped without a
byte-identical null, so it is used only for the read:write ratio.

## Study 40 commissioned — decide the two GATED arms

Not a hunt for new levers. **Two changes are built, gated, and fully measured on
every counter except the one that decides them:**

| arm | state | what is missing |
|---|---|---|
| **`insertwin34`** (study 34) | node-identical, `Bench` bit-for-bit, **+0.702% instructions for −0.530% branches and −4% of ALL engine mispredicts** | a cycles verdict on the trade |
| **`null38`** / the `thr_copy` remainder (study 38) | site measured at 63.7 i/n, alias banked 17.1, machinery costs +19.7 | **~26 i/n with no mechanism** |

Both authors stopped at *"cycles cannot adjudicate this"* — **correct at the
time.** It is no longer true. Two instruments now exist that they did not have:

1. **The fixed-node cycles frame** — fixed NODE budget, validate node *and*
   instruction counts before trusting the delta, alternate order, report **rank
   separation** rather than spread (common-mode noise cancels; independent noise
   does not).
2. **Study 39's differencing trick** — a clean number built as the **difference
   of two individually broken arms** (`dstpin` − `actpin0`), which is precisely
   how it beat a question two studies had deferred.

The brief is explicit that **a measured null is a success** — *"arm A is +0.05%
cycles, here is the panel and its null, leave it off"* closes the question —
but that **leaving both switches in the state it found them is not.** It also
carries the caveat that arm A *deliberately* changes instructions, so its frame
validates on nodes only and the instruction delta must be reasoned about rather
than expected to vanish.

Context worth recording: **nobody has ever landed a mispredict-targeted change
in this engine**, and `init_quiets`' insertion walk plus exit is **6.46% of ALL
engine branch mispredicts in a single branch**.

## Recode POC COMPLETE and MERGED (`af05c2c`) — phase 1 says YES

6 commits. One conflict in `sota_features.rs` (the feature-index code) resolved
and inspected by hand — the additions are the `ThreatRemap` type and the
resolver's `remap` field; `gatepoc` is running to confirm behaviour via the
eval-hash pin.

### Phase-1 verdict: the smaller table DOES buy cycles — small but real

**77.7 MB → 10.5 MB threat table: ~−1.5% cycles (paired median) under typical
conditions, −3%…−16% under memory pressure.** Sign is consistent across every
battery: **32/42 rounds favour the small table, sign-test p ≈ 4·10⁻⁴.** At
~120 Elo/doubling that is **≈ +2.5 Elo quiet, more under load — and match
conditions ARE loaded** (concurrent games).

**The frame validity is the best of the session** — it executed the commissioned
recipe exactly:

    same binary; two eval-bit-identical nets (both hash 591b92e1... on wl50)
    differing ONLY in threat-table size; fixed 100k-node budget
    node totals identical across all 28 runs (5,001,626)
    instruction medians agree to 14 instructions in 28.2 BILLION (-0.0000%)

⇒ cycles genuinely was the only free variable.

**Mechanism, and it redirects the next lever:** LLC misses fell only **~1.75%**.
The residual miss population is **HalfKA (28.8 MB) + TT + PSQ1**, which
partially confirms the design study's §8.1 suspicion and points the next step at
**HalfKA coarsening** — getting the whole input under L3 — rather than more
threat work. **Dose response: R'=4096 ≈ R'=8192, so most of the win is already
collected at 8192 rows.**

**Side benefits measured, not assumed:** a **69 MB smaller net**, and a
**~316M-cycle / 235M-instruction cheaper process launch** (the loader checksums
the whole payload). Loads are **12.2% of an SPSA unit's instructions**, so this
matters for screen and tuning throughput independently of search speed.

### What landed

- **Engine:** TRM1 CENN section + compressed threat tables, remap applied at the
  **single `ThreatResolver::index_decoded` chokepoint** shared by the
  incremental, refresh and PSQ1 paths — so they cannot drift. **Nets without
  TRM1 are bit-identical**, gate green (`Bench: 587683`, eval hash
  `2e1ede95…`, d13 `1739588`). New tests include a compressed-vs-scattered game
  walk with the debug oracle at every node.
- **Phase 2:** trainer-side remap (`TrainConfig.threat_remap`,
  `ArchSpec.threat_rows`, `map_batch(threat_remap=)`) with **zero trainer-graph
  changes**, verified index-exact on real records. Worktree `jax/` only — **the
  live D49 trainer is untouched.**
- **Phase 3 built and ARMED:** `recode-t1.service` is **live now**, waiting for
  D49 (~sb350/400) **plus 10 idle-GPU minutes**, then runs identity → random8k →
  kmeans8k sequentially with the **pre-registered decision rule in the
  docstring**. Its own CPU smoke: projection costs +5.3% relative one-batch
  heldout MSE before finetune — the quantity the panel measures recovery of.
  **Declared deviation:** the fly-hash k=2 control is deferred (needs 2× active
  index padding); the three arms the rule consumes are all present.

### OPERATIONAL TRAP: `refs/stash` is SHARED ACROSS WORKTREES

The POC reported popping another worktree's stash by mistake. **Checked: both
entries are intact and nothing was lost** — and `stash@{0}` is already labelled
*"RESTORED by perf-study-24: popped by mistake… refs/stash is shared across
worktrees"*. **Two separate studies have now hit this.** It is a real trap, not
a one-off: `git stash` is global even when worktrees are not. Future briefs
should say so.

**Its own least-sure list:** whether 12 low-LR superbatches recover the
projection cost (if all arms fail the rule, the pre-registered answer is
**report-and-pivot, not rule-stretching**); the k-means metric (L2 in row space)
— suspect it first if kmeans ≈ random; which load regime prices the speed win
for real matches; and that the census used one corpus band, not the training
mix.

**`gatefix` green — all three pins bit-for-bit:**

    eval hash : 2e1ede9521624dd89...93cf79c4   (unchanged)
    d13 nodes : 1739588                        (unchanged)
    Bench     : 587683
    All checks passed.   GATE_EXIT=0

**So the TRM1 merge IS behaviour-preserving for non-TRM1 nets**, and the
double-fold my keep-both resolver introduced is gone. `gate39` had already
passed independently, confirming study 39's merge was clean and the damage was
mine alone, in the POC merge.

### The incident, recorded for the process rather than the code

`af05c2c` resolved a `sota_features.rs` conflict with a script because it
reported **a single hunk**. That script concatenated two versions of
`refresh_threat_into_at` — study 39's `foldcount` block with 3-arg calls, then
the POC's `match remap` with its 4-arg call. It **failed to compile (E0061)**,
which is how it was caught, **and the compile error was masking a correctness
bug**: had the arity happened to match, the function would have folded every
threat row **twice**.

**Rule I am adopting: a keep-both resolver is fine for docs and `.gitignore`,
and unsafe for code.** The three earlier code conflicts this session were
inspected by hand and were fine; this one I delegated purely because it looked
small. Size is not the criterion — file type is.

| | sb390 | **sb400** |
|---|---:|---:|
| raw | 0.010824 | **0.010784** |
| ema | 0.010812 | 0.010804 |

is the completion of the convergence traced all night (bonus 47 → 26 → 19 → 9 →
2 → **negative**): as LR → 0 the EMA has nothing left to average over, and the
live weights caught and passed it.

It is only **~1.8 Elo** by the map — below screen resolution — but it means the
**"deploy the EMA" guard I encoded in `d49_verdict.sh` is no longer
automatically right.** The live-weight net should be carried as a verdict
candidate rather than assumed inferior.
