## Tick — 2026-07-31 13:31, worktree prune: 66 removed, ~12 GB freed — AND I DELETED MY OWN WORKTREE

**80 worktrees had accumulated**, 56 of them agent worktrees holding **8.0 GB**.
`git worktree prune` reported **zero** prunable — every one was still on disk, so
nothing would have been reclaimed automatically.

Pruned by **merge status**, not age or name: removed only where `HEAD` is an
ancestor of `main`. **66 removed, 153 GB → 165 GB.** Largest single reclaim was
`perf-study-52` at **2.9 GB** of stale `target/`. Kept the 11 unmerged worktrees
(`reprgap-seam`, `conj-study`, `perf-study-45`, six agent trees) — **unmerged
means unlanded work and none of it was mine to discard** — and excluded study 59's
active worktree *by name before the loop*, not by trusting the merge test.

### The bug in my own filter, and what it cost

**`chessengine-mainwt` — my dedicated main worktree — was itself deleted.** Its
`HEAD` *is* `main`, so it trivially passed "is an ancestor of main". The exclusion
I wrote protected the running agent and forgot the one worktree I use every tick.

The consequence was the exact failure that worktree exists to prevent: my next
`cd` failed silently, the shell stayed in the shared checkout, and `git add -A`
+ commit put the handoff **on `repr-gap-study`** — the wrong-branch problem this
setup was built to solve, reintroduced by deleting the solution.

**Repaired, and verified rather than assumed at each step:**

1. Checked what the stray commit actually contained. `git add -A` had swept up
   dirty files in the shared checkout — `screen.sh` (+24), `d49_verdict.sh` (+13).
   **`git diff main <stray> -- tools/screen.sh tools/d49_verdict.sh` is EMPTY**:
   those were diffs against `repr-gap-study`'s stale copies, not new work. Nothing
   was lost by discarding the commit.
2. Recreated the worktree at `main`.
3. Reset `repr-gap-study` back to `1b3845ec`, its own last real commit.

**Two rules from this.** An exclusion list must name **every** worktree that must
survive, and "is merged" is exactly the wrong test for a *working* tree — being
identical to main is what a working tree is *supposed* to look like. And a `cd`
into a deleted directory fails silently while the rest of a `&&`-free script runs
on regardless: **build a script's later steps on the `cd` succeeding, or check
`pwd`.**

**D49 verified unaffected after deleting 66 directories** — sb1025, 193k pos/s,
four services active. Its checkpoints live under `data/`, not in any worktree, but
that is worth confirming rather than assuming.

**D49:** heldout ema **0.010641** at sb1020, the run's lowest — still inside the
sawtooth, still not a trend, unreadable until sb1400 (~13 h out).
**Study 59** working, no builds in flight.

## Tick — 2026-07-31 13:47, the prune's second-order cost: it destroyed build artifacts

Integrity confirmed after yesterday's repair: shared checkout back on
`repr-gap-study @ 1b3845ec`, `mainwt` on `main @ 00cfbc6b`, D49 untouched at
sb1031.

**But the prune cost more than the worktrees.** Going to price the full 52–58
series with the new `tools/wallclock_panel.py`, **both PGO binaries were gone**:

- `chessengine-pre52` was itself pruned — its `HEAD` (`f289433c`) is an ancestor
  of `main`, so it passed the same "is merged" test that ate `mainwt`.
- `archon-58-pgo` vanished when I **recreated `mainwt`**: `engines/bin/` is
  gitignored, so `git worktree add` materialises it **empty**. Same mechanism that
  broke the first PGO build attempt this morning (`cp: cannot create regular file
  'engines/bin/archon-pre52-pgo'`), arriving from the other direction.

**A "merged" worktree is not a disposable worktree.** Its tracked content is
reproducible from git; its **gitignored build output — target dirs, PGO binaries,
profile data — is not.** Those cost 10+ minutes each to regenerate. The merge test
says nothing about them.

**What saved it: copies in the scratchpad.** `pgo_null` is **1,246,112 bytes —
byte-for-byte the size of `archon-pre52-pgo`** — because I had copied it out when
building the earlier panel. The reference half of the pair survived; only the
post-58 half needed rebuilding. **Preserving a binary outside the tree that
produced it turned a 20-minute loss into a 10-minute one**, and it is why the
comparison is still possible at all.

**Rebuilding `archon-58-pgo` at `main` now**, then running the 40-round panel to
price studies **52–58** shipped. Expected input: the shipped instruction delta is

priced 52–56 only, at **−1.020% wall-clock → +1.77 DERIVED Elo**.

The rebuild script now **gates every later step on the `cd` succeeding and asserts

failure that put a commit on the wrong branch.

Rebuilt `archon-58-pgo` at `main` and ran `tools/wallclock_panel.py` (40 rounds)
against the preserved `archon-pre52-pgo`:

```
NULL (byte-identical) : median +0.204%  sd 6.734  sign 19/40 neg  p=0.8746
ARM                   : median -0.909%  sd 6.760  sign 26/40 neg  p=0.0807
LICENSED (median-only, pre-registered)  ->  -0.909%  ->  +1.58 DERIVED Elo
```

### Licence and significance are different questions, and only one passed cleanly

The pre-registered criterion licences the **instrument** (|null| < |arm|/2 — it
can resolve an effect this size). It says nothing about whether *this* effect is
real. **The arm's own sign test is p = 0.0807 — marginal, and much weaker than the
52–56 panel's p = 0.0064** (29/40 vs 26/40). The `sd` is also higher, 6.76 vs
4.99, because study 59 was building through part of the run.

So: **the instrument worked; the reading is soft.** I am recording +1.58 as
DERIVED *and* marginal, not as a firmed-up number.

### The observation that matters more than the Elo

| series | shipped i/n | wall-clock | sign p | derived Elo |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 52–56 | −162.4 | −1.020% | 0.0064 | +1.77 |
| **52–58** | **−317.7** | **−0.909%** | 0.0807 | **+1.58** |

**Shipped instructions nearly DOUBLED (−162.4 → −317.7) and wall-clock did not
move.** Taken at face value it went slightly the wrong way, but −1.020% and
−0.909% are well inside each other's noise, so the honest reading is: **studies 57
and 58 added ~155 i/n of shipped instruction reduction with no measurable
wall-clock effect.**

That is a real, uncomfortable observation and it sharpens the earlier finding.
Instructions do not merely *overstate* time by a constant 2.63× — **the marginal
conversion for these particular later arms is indistinguishable from zero.** The
engine at this point is not instruction-throughput bound in the way the gate
assumes.

**Caveat stated rather than buried:** the `Ptnml [0,0,750,0,0]` node-null was run
on the pre-52 ↔ post-56 pair, **not** this one. Studies 57 and 58 were each gated
node-identical, so it should hold transitively, but that is inference, not the
measurement. The Elo conversion inherits that caveat.

**Next instrument question, to be pre-registered before the next panel, not
after:** whether the licence should require the arm's sign test to reach p<0.05 as
well as the median criterion. This run is exactly the case that motivates it —
**and that is why it does not get applied retroactively here.**

## Tick — 2026-07-31 14:1x, WHY THE INSTRUCTIONS DIDN'T CONVERT: ARCHON IS MEMORY-BOUND

Last tick's uncomfortable observation — ~155 shipped i/n added with no wall-clock
movement — has a measured cause. Profiled `archon-58-pgo` (shipped config,
canonical net, pinned frame):

| metric | value |
|---|---|
| **IPC** | **1.595** — Zen 4 realistic peak ~3–4 |
| **L1d load miss rate** | **18.33%** — 2.71 **billion** misses, **543 per node** |
| branch miss rate | 4.07% → ~**9.4% of cycles** at a 15-cycle penalty |
| instructions/node | 5724 |
| **stall share of cycles** | **47%** (IPC 3) · **60%** (IPC 4) · 73% (peak 6) |

**At minimum half the engine's time is stall, not instruction issue.** Even on the
most conservative assumption about achievable IPC, instruction issue is under half
the budget. **That is why −317.7 shipped i/n bought nothing measurable.**

### What this means for the programme

**The perf programme has been optimising the thing that is not binding.** Seven
studies, all gated on `instructions:u`, all landing real instruction reductions —
and the shipped wall-clock gain across the whole series is ~**−0.9%**, marginal at
p=0.081.

**`instructions:u` should stay the gate.** It is the only counter with a 0.000
null, it is load-robust and cheap, and it reliably answers *"did this arm do what
I meant?"*. **What it does not answer is *"is this arm worth anything?"** — and
the programme has been treating one as the other. Those are separate claims and
they now have separate instruments: `instructions:u` for the first,
`tools/wallclock_panel.py` for the second.

### Where the next studies should aim

1. **Cache/memory locality — 543 L1d misses per node.** The standing note that
   **65.75% of cache misses sit in the three accumulator-fold kernels** has been
   in the directive all along and is now the *primary* target rather than a
   footnote. This is where the stall is.
2. **Branch mispredicts — ~9.4% of cycles** at 4.07% of 2.76 B branches. Second
   largest single identified block.

Study 59 is already in flight against `search::<NonPv>` / `at_full` /
`init_quiets` on an instruction-weighted profile. **It is not being redirected
mid-run** — its targets are large and its arms will be measured properly — but the
brief for study 60 should lead with memory locality and require a wall-clock panel
before any arm is called valuable.

**Honest framing:** this does not retract the landed work. −486.8 i/n on the gate
build is real, node-identical, and gate-green, and a faster-issuing engine is not
worse. It says the *pricing* was wrong, that ~+1.6 DERIVED Elo is what seven
studies bought, and that the next seven should be aimed somewhere else.

**D49:** sb1045, 193k pos/s, four services active.

(`archon-58-pgo`, canonical net, pinned frame):

| `eval::evaluate` | 2.97 |

**Three accumulator-fold kernels = 54.96% of all L1d misses; 61.93% with
`finny_refresh_into`.**

**The directive's standing claim — "65.75% of cache misses are in the three
accumulator-fold kernels" — essentially SURVIVES re-measurement** (54.96% for
three, 61.93% including the fourth fold kernel). That is worth recording plainly:
I have corrected several directive claims today (the stale profile list, the
non-existent `i16_deltas_into`), and **this one holds.** It has also been sitting
in the directive as a footnote the whole time while the programme optimised
instruction counts.

### The framing that makes study 60 worth running

**`forward_into` is #1 in cache misses at 20.44%, and perf study 58 correctly
concluded there is no cheap INSTRUCTION win left in it** — activate 35%, l1_dot
30%, l2_tail 13%, find_nnz 12%, glue 10%, every component on a documented floor.

**A function closed for instruction work can be wide open for memory work.**
Those studies measured a different thing. The brief says so explicitly, so study
58's conclusion is not inherited as a reason to skip the biggest miss source in
the engine.

**Study 60 commissioned** on memory locality in the four fold kernels, with
branch mispredicts (~9.4% of cycles) as the fallback target. Its brief inverts the
programme's instrument hierarchy:

- **`instructions:u` is demoted to a GUARD** — it answers "did my arm do what I
  meant", not "is this worth anything". A memory arm may *raise* instruction count
  and still win.
- **`tools/wallclock_panel.py` is mandatory** — no arm may be called valuable
  without a 40-round panel result.
- Cache counters must ship their own published null (`ls_stlf` **fails** its;

## Tick — 2026-07-31, study 59 merged; IT RETRACTED ITS OWN ELO AND HALVED MINE

**Merged `86d5762a`, gate green on the rebased tree.** Two arms: `lutp`
(`index_decoded` reads one table where it read two) and `qrow` (`quantise_halfka`
becomes a row walk LLVM auto-vectorises). **Shipped −176.77 i/n (−3.086%)**,
gate −26.97.

Its wall-clock panel **passed the stricter sign test I proposed one tick earlier**
— null +0.010% (20/40, p=1.0000), arm −0.888% (30/40, **p=0.0022**) → +1.54
DERIVED Elo.

### And then it retracted that number itself, in three places

**"+1.54 is a fixed-node-PROCESS number, not a game number."** Two independent
measurements put **~94% of its cycles delta in fixed STARTUP cost** (1-node panel:
−147.2 M/process against −156.8 M for the whole frame). The residual is ~1.9
cycles/node of 3538 — below this box's floor. **At a real TC the expectation is
~0.**

**I verified it against my own pair, and it halves my headline too:**

| | cycles |
|---|---|
| startup-only delta (1 node) | **−261.4 M** |
| full-frame delta (5M nodes) | −526.0 M |
| **startup share of the 52–58 gain** | **49.7%** |
| search-only residual | −52.9 cycles/node of 3809 (**−1.39%**) |

**The 5M-node bench frame charges process startup ONCE PER RUN.** A real game
amortises it over minutes of search. So **+1.77 and +1.58 DERIVED Elo are roughly
double their game-relevant value** — study 59's arms were 94% startup because they
targeted `quantise_halfka` directly; the series as a whole is ~half. Both are
single-shot cycles reads, so 49.7% is indicative, not precise — but two
independent routes agree on the direction.

**Fixed in the instrument, not just the doc:** `tools/wallclock_panel.py` gains
`--startup-frame`, which runs the identical panel at 1 node so the fixed component
can be measured and subtracted, with the docstring stating that **the residual is
the game-relevant number.**

### Four findings from the study

1. **The profile "disagreement" I handed over was PGO, not error.** Both tables
   reproduce; PGO folds `do_move` into `search::<NonPv>` (13.50→26.01%) and the
   fold kernels into `current_full` (2.36→8.73%). My brief's "5834.4 i/n on the
   gate build" is the **v3** baseline while its build scripts default to
   **native** — the same two-compilers confusion, one level up.
2. **`perf record -e instructions:u` is NOT usable per-instruction or per-line on
   this box.** One stack load collects 13.32% of `search::<NonPv>`'s samples; one
   `prefetcht0` collects 13.15% of the *engine's*. Study 36 §2.3 killed
   per-mnemonic attribution; this is the same defect two granularities finer.
   Replacement is **AMD IBS** — and `lutp` exists ONLY because IBS put one source
   line at 0.90% of the engine.
3. **The target-cpu rule reproduces with its mechanism in the disassembly, and
   points the OTHER WAY.** `qrow` is **14.1× LARGER on native** than v3, because
   AVX-512DQ has `vpmullq` and AVX2 does not; `lutp` is 0.76×. **There is no
   constant correction factor between the two targets — it is per-arm and it can
   invert.** My "v3 overstates" rule was generalised from study 57's arms alone.
4. **Re-checking a stale banner means reading what it said the CAUSE was.** Study
   57's "do not re-propose the row-major rewrite" is honoured: its §4.4 blamed
   `Vec::extend` losing its bulk path to a `FlatMap`, and `qrow` does not collect
   at all. The arm that KEPT the iterator measured −29.31; the one that DROPPED it
   measured **−158.27**.

**Negatives with numbers:** no arm landed in `search::<NonPv>`, the largest
target — every sub-block has a prior study's number against it, and study 52's
spill-class figure reproduces (19.0% vs 19.3%). `correction_value` lazy ≈4 i/n;
`reduction`'s `idiv` declined (a reciprocal costs *more*); node-entry init ~7.6
i/n but needs `MaybeUninit`. The `copy_nonoverlapping` at 7.52% of `at_full` is
`core::arch`'s SIMD load/store, **not a memcpy** — an arm aimed at it would have
found nothing.

**Correction to my brief, from the study:** it did **not** re-derive my
"−30.8 native / −187.6 v3" figures — those are merged into its base. **Its 6×
receipt is independent, not a confirmation of mine.**

**Incidental and valuable:** `build_pgo.sh` is **byte-reproducible across
invocations** — a pair rebuilt hours later with independently retrained profiles
reproduces the earlier `.text` md5s exactly. **That retires study 52 §7 item 3's
standing caveat** that PGO retraining is uncharacterised variation in every PGO
comparison in the repo.

### Instrument note added to memory: cycles is not load-robust

The σ = 1.57% figure for `cycles:u` was measured on a **quiet** box. Today the
same binary read **17.95 B cycles quiet and 26.92 B under a match plus a build:
+49.9%**, while its `instructions:u` drifted **+0.08%** across the same two runs.

That is why the attempt to verify study 59's "94% startup" returned 4.6% — the
answer was measuring the contention, not the arm. **Never take a cycles reading
while a match, a build or another study is running, and not even a paired one:
the inflation lives in the stall component and does not cancel.** Wall-clock
panels survive load only because `tools/wallclock_panel.py` interleaves the null
*within* each round.

## Tick — 2026-07-31, study 60 merged: NO arm landed, and it found a live PGO corruption

**Merged `220106b4`, gate green.** Three arms built, all node-identical and
eval-bit-exact, **all lost**. Its value is elsewhere.

### The headline is a bug fix that has been live since study 40

**`engines/canonical/net.cenn` is a symlink into gitignored `data/`, so it DANGLES
in every fresh git worktree — which is where every perf study since 40 has run.**
`build_pgo.sh`'s own header documents this failure class *in capitals* and had **no
check behind it**: the UCI training session ran with `setoption` failing, the
profile merged, and a binary came out, silently.

| build (5,001,562-node frame, median of 3) | i/node | vs non-PGO |
|---|---:|---:|
| non-PGO native | 5819.68 | — |
| **PGO trained WITH the net** | **5728.98** | −1.559% |
| **PGO trained WITHOUT the net** | **6040.90** | **+3.801%** |

**It is not "PGO inert" as the header supposed — a netless PGO binary is 5.445%
worse than the correct one, and worse than no PGO at all.** The script now
**refuses** to emit a binary whose eval-file never loaded.

### My own PGO numbers are unaffected — verified two ways, not assumed

1. **Mechanism:** my builds passed a **resolved absolute path**
   (`/home/nick/projects/chessengine/data/…/s600.cenn`), not the symlink, and
   `build_pgo.sh` substitutes that straight into `setoption`.
2. **Outcome:** my post-58 PGO measures **5729.0 i/n** against this study's
   correct-PGO **5728.98** — matching to **0.02 i/n**.

So "native+PGO absorbs 38%", the −317.7 shipped i/n, and both wall-clock panels
stand. **Studies that passed the bare `engines/canonical/net.cenn` path from
inside a worktree are the ones at risk** — that is a question for the archive,
not for today's numbers.

### Where my brief was wrong, with receipts

Its profile replicates exactly (63.19% of L1d misses in four accumulator symbols).
**Its premise does not.** "Nobody has attacked this as a memory problem" is
**false**: study 51 already measured the ceiling — **`probe-wpin` = −13.19%
cycles, 15/15, p=0.0001** — and **nine routes to it are closed with numbers**
(eight prefetch arms, zero-rows 30-of-19.6M, nibble-packing 0.03%, cancellation
1.99%, huge pages, 4 KB congruence, fusion, hardware pollution control).

**And my ranking was wrong.** Ranked by miss **latency** rather than miss
**count** — study 29 §8's rule, which I did not apply — **`forward_into` is 11.8%
while the fold's weight rows are 55.05%.** I handed over a count-ranked table and
called it the target list. It also computed a floor nobody had written down: the
fold's own vector arithmetic is **~287 cycles/node ≈ 8% of the frame** that no
locality work can touch.

**That is my brief's premise refuted for the fourth study running** — and each
time the study was right to check rather than accept it.

### Instrument reality, corroborating `c09e21be` independently

`cycles:u`'s per-round spread was **1.6% in both panels, 5× the standing quiet-box
σ**, and **`wallclock_panel.py` REFUSED (NOT LICENSED)** even on a contrast with a
**+5.445%** instruction difference. Two independent routes on the same afternoon
now say cycles and wall-clock are unusable on a loaded box. **No number in study
60 is quoted on cycles or wall-clock.**

**Two process failures it recorded rather than hid:** it hoisted a `let` outside a
`#[cfg]` and changed the **control** binary's `.text` md5 (caught by rebuilding,
not by mtime); and it ran **`git stash`** while a panel was appending inside the
tree, voiding a 20-minute panel. **This repo's standing rule is never `git
stash`** — `refs/stash` is shared across worktrees.

## Tick — 2026-07-31, PGO-corruption audit + study 61 commissioned on study 51's ceiling

### Audit of the dangling-net bug across the archive

Study 60's guard is on main and is the right shape: **`[ -r ]` on the eval-file
(the exact dangling-symlink test) plus a grep for `ERROR EvalFile` in the training
session** — belt and braces, because a path can exist and still fail to load.

**What the archive shows:** studies **17, 18 and 19** document the invocation with
the **bare** `engines/canonical/net.cenn` path, e.g.
`bash tools/build_pgo.sh sota,arch-d48x engines/bin/archon-s17 engines/canonical/net.cenn`.
In a worktree that path dangles. **Those appear in next-steps lists, so they may
never have been executed** — I am not asserting those studies are corrupted, only
that the recipe they wrote down is the broken one.

**Study 18's "recorded trap", referenced by study 19, is a DIFFERENT bug** —
`build_pgo.sh` prepending `engines/bin/` to its second argument, so an absolute
*output* path fails the copy. The dangling *net* is genuinely new to study 60.

**Scoped honestly:** determining whether any specific historical binary was built
netless requires rebuilding it at its commit, which is a build each and settles
nothing today's numbers depend on. **Today's numbers are verified clean** (resolved
absolute path; post-58 PGO at 5729.0 against study 60's correct-PGO 5728.98).
Recorded as an archive question with the test that would settle it, not launched
as a retroactive sweep.

**One item worth an owner's decision:** the canonical match engine is
`engines/canonical/archon -> ../bin/archon-s25`. If it was built netless it is
~5.4% slower than it should be, and rebuilding would be a free speedup for every
match. Determining that needs a build at study 25's commit; **promoting a
rebuilt canonical engine is a promotion decision, not a maintenance one**, so it
is flagged rather than done.

### Study 61 commissioned — at the largest measured prize in the engine

**Study 51's `probe-wpin` measured −13.19% cycles, 15/15, p=0.0001.** That is the
biggest single measured opportunity anywhere in ARCHON, it is a *memory*
opportunity, and **nine routes to it are closed with numbers** — eight prefetch
arms plus zero-rows, nibble-packing, cancellation, huge pages, 4 KB congruence,
fusion, pollution control, and study 60's three.

The brief tells it to read all nine first, **not to re-propose prefetching**
(nine attempts is enough), and states plainly that **"the ceiling is unreachable"
is not a finding, not a deliverable and not an acceptable conclusion** — with this
project's own receipt attached: a prior study priced an "unavoidable" gap at
325–740 units when the real figure was 197.7.

It carries the corrected ranking (**miss LATENCY, not count** — the fold's weight
rows are 55.05%, `forward_into` 11.8%), study 60's ~287 cycles/node arithmetic
floor, the memory-bound evidence (IPC 1.595, ≥47% stall), and every instrument
change from today: cycles is **not** load-robust (+49.9% under contention),
`instructions:u` is a guard not a licence, `wallclock_panel.py` is mandatory and
has `--startup-frame`, IBS replaces per-line `perf record`, and the `build_pgo.sh`
net trap with its resolved-path fix.

## Tick — 2026-07-31 15:42, canonical-engine question sharpened (and I nearly mis-corrected myself)

I set out to walk back last tick's flag on `archon-s25`, on a recollection that
the canonical engine "predates CEN5 and cannot load the current net" — which would
have made the netless-PGO question moot. **I checked instead of asserting, and the
recollection was wrong.**

```
archon-s25 + canonical net  ->  info string eval: NNUE     (loads fine)
```

The CEN5 incompatibility I was remembering applies to **D49's packed nets**, which
is why `d49_verdict.sh` carries its `ENGINE=` warning — **not** to the canonical
net. **So last tick's flag stands and my doubt was the error.** Worth recording:
the near-miss was a *correct* statement about to be overwritten by a misremembered
one, and the only thing that stopped it was running the probe.

**The question is therefore live, and slightly larger than I said.**
`engines/canonical/archon` is the default engine for **five tools** —
`screen.sh`, `paired_nps.py`, `frame_compare.py`, `eval_bench.py`,
`reprgap/twins.py`. If it was built netless it is ~5.4% slower than it should be.

**Impact is uneven, and that matters for how urgent this is:**

- `screen.sh` is **fixed-node**, so a slower engine changes nothing there — every
  D49 Elo number in this document is unaffected. (Our screens also override
  `ENGINE=` anyway.)
- **`paired_nps.py` measures speed directly** and would inherit the full 5.4%.
  Its own header exists because match-aggregated NPS confounded two engines
  "within 0.7%" into "1.237× apart" — so it is precisely the tool where a silently
  degraded baseline would do damage.

**What would settle it: one PGO build at `archon-s25`'s commit with the now-guarded
script, compared against a non-PGO native build of the same commit.** Study 60's
discriminator is unambiguous — correct PGO reads **−1.559%** against non-PGO
native, netless reads **+3.801%**. **`engines/canonical/` records no build
commit** (`archon`, `arch.txt`, `MANIFEST.md`, `net.cenn` — no provenance line),
so the commit has to be recovered before that test can run.

**Left as an owner decision, unchanged:** promoting a rebuilt canonical engine is
a promotion, not maintenance. **Added as a concrete gap:** `engines/canonical/`
should record the commit its binary was built from — the absence of that line is
what makes this question expensive instead of trivial, and it is the same
provenance-by-artifact problem that produced today's phantom −0.473%.

## Tick — 2026-07-31 15:5x, D49's cyclical schedule VERIFIED APPLIED (10.93σ)

With ~11 h to the readable point, I had never confirmed the trainer is applying
the specified schedule rather than merely carrying it. **Both halves now checked.**

**Half 1 — the config matches the specification element for element:**

| specified | `d49y_cyc_1280.json` |
|---|---|
| 1000 more on top of D49's 700 | `superbatches 1700`, `lr_end_superbatch 700` ✓ |
| 35 cycles × 20 sb | **35 peaks**, spaced 20 ✓ |
| 1e-4 down to 5e-6 | `[700, 1e-4] … [719, 5e-6]` ✓ |
| peak decay **0.985**/cycle | 9.85e-5 / 1e-4 = **0.9850** ✓ |
| at sb1400 jump to 5e-5, anneal to zero over 300 | `[1400, 5e-5] … [1700, 1e-7]` ✓ |

**Half 2 — the trainer does not log LR, so it was inferred by phase-lock.**
Across all 395 superbatches since sb701:

| | train loss | n |
|---|---|---|
| near cycle **peak** (high LR) | 0.007158 | 59 |
| near cycle **trough** (low LR) | 0.007040 | 57 |
| **difference** | **+0.000118 ± 0.000011** | **10.93σ** |

**A sawtooth phase-locked to the 20-superbatch boundary cannot arise unless the
cyclical LR is being applied**, and the sign is correct — high LR raises training
loss. Neither half would have sufficed alone: **a config can be right and
ignored, and a loss curve can drift for unrelated reasons.** The phase-lock is
what ties the two together.

That also explains, mechanically, why every mid-cycling Elo point has landed
modestly behind canonical: those checkpoints are sampled at arbitrary phases of a
sawtooth whose peaks demonstrably *worsen* the fit. It is a reason to keep
refusing to read the experiment before sb1400, not a reason to worry.

**Left inconclusive, honestly:** the `archon-s25` netless-PGO question. A
structural test — are the NNUE kernels banished to a cold text section, as they
would be if never profiled? — **does not discriminate**; both the canonical binary
and a known-good PGO build expose a single `.text`. Without its build commit the
question cannot be settled, so I stopped rather than over-read a null test. The
requirement to record commit + build command + `.text` md5 for any future
canonical promotion is now in `engines/canonical/MANIFEST.md`.

**D49:** sb1094, 193k pos/s, four services active. **Study 61** working.

## Tick — 2026-07-31 16:16, CORRECTION: the heldout EMA *does* have a trend (t = −6.83)

I have written "still inside the sawtooth, still not a trend" at nearly every tick
since sb900. **I finally tested it instead of repeating it, and it is wrong.**

Regression over all 40 EMA points since sb700 (sb710–sb1100):

| | |
|---|---|
| slope | **−2.483e-07 per superbatch** |
| se | 3.634e-08 |
| **t** | **−6.83 — SIGNIFICANT** |
| projected over the 400 sb measured | **−0.000099 of EMA** |
| EMA spread | 0.010620 … 0.010765 |
| residual sd after removing the trend | **0.000026** |

The residual sd is small against the spread, so the trend accounts for a large
share of the movement — this is not a line fitted through noise.

**How the error happened, because it is the reusable part.** The claim was
*defensible* when I first made it at n≈4: three or four readings of a sawtooth
genuinely cannot show a trend. It became false as points accumulated, and **I
carried it forward as a stock phrase instead of re-running the test.** A caveat
that was true once does not stay true; **a claim repeated across ticks needs
re-testing at the new n, not restating.**

### What it does and does not mean

**It is NOT a strength claim.** Loss is not Elo — the standing receipt is that
**44e-6 of heldout moved alongside ~30 Elo of games**, an order of magnitude off
any exchange rate. −99e-6 over 400 superbatches converts to nothing.

**What it is worth:**

1. **The schedule is doing something.** Combined with the phase-lock result
   (peaks vs troughs, 10.93σ), the cycling is both *applied* and *productive* —
   the net is still learning at sb1100, not oscillating in place.
2. **The abort condition is comfortably not firing**, and now on a fitted trend
   rather than on eyeballed readings.

**Neither changes the reading rule:** the experiment is still arbitrated by games
at **sb1400** and **sb1700**, and every mid-cycling Elo point remains sampled at
an arbitrary phase of a sawtooth whose peaks measurably worsen the fit.

`med61.py`, so it is on IBS and cache fills as briefed.

## Tick — 2026-07-31 16:31, re-tested the OTHER standing claims; found a double-count in my own pooling

Last tick's EMA correction raised an obvious question: **what else am I repeating
without re-testing?** So I re-derived the other standing claim.

**Claim: "mid-cycling is statistically indistinguishable from sb700."**
**It survives** — but the number I had been quoting was built wrongly.

| | |
|---|---|
| mid-cycling, **clean** (sb900 + sb1060, 3000 games) | **−10.52 ± 8.05** |
| vs sb700 (−4.14 ± 5.56) | −6.38 ± 9.79 = **0.65σ** |

**The double-count.** I reported **−12.66 ± 5.88** by inverse-variance pooling
"prior mid-cycling pooled (incl. sb900) −13.84 ± 6.89" with sb1060 — but that
prior figure *already contained* sb900, and was itself built on a
"−15.1 ± 8.6" whose composition I never set. **Pooling a pool with one of its own
members double-counts it and shrinks the error bar illegitimately** — which is
exactly why −12.66 ± 5.88 looked tighter than the clean −10.52 ± 8.05 despite
resting on no more games.

This is the project's own rule 8 — *re-derive any number before building on it,
especially one whose `n` you did not set* — and I violated it while quoting the
result across several ticks. **The operational conclusion does not change** (0.65σ
vs 1.05σ, both far from significant), so nothing downstream was misled; the fix is
to quote the clean figure from here.

**Method note added to memory** as the complement to "quote levels, not trends":
both failures — over-reading noise and repeating a stale caveat — are the same
error of **quoting a conclusion instead of computing one**, and they point in
opposite directions, so neither "be more cautious" nor "be more decisive" is the
fix. Re-run the test when `n` has grown.

**D49:** sb1109, 193k pos/s, four services active, 156 GB disk. **Study 61**
building.

## Tick — 2026-07-31, study 61 merged: −68.87 i/n AND the tenth route closed with a model

**Merged `d99d537f`. Gate green** (`All checks passed.`, `Bench: 587683`), verified
from my own v3 build: **post-58 5834.4 → post-61 5760.1 i/n = −74.4** for studies
59 + 61 combined, against a claimed sum of −95.84 — **78% realised, non-additive
as this series keeps showing.**

### The win is a stale `match` arm — the series' recurring shape, again

`i8_deltas_into_inner`'s shape dispatch matches four `(adds, subs)` pairs chosen
in **study 25, when the kernel was HalfKA-only** — where those four are a
*complete* cover. **Study 32 then routed the entire FullThreats channel through
the same dispatch and nobody re-read the `match`.** Counting the threat channel's
pairs for the first time: **19.11% of threat folds are a SINGLE weight row running
the runtime-length generic kernel.** Straight-line coverage 27.27% → **68.40%**.

That is now four studies whose best arm came from re-reading a decision made under
assumptions a later change invalidated (37→57, 25→61, and study 58's inverse).

### THE TENTH ROUTE IS CLOSED — with a validated model, not a shrug

Two new instruments — a **fill-SOURCE census** (L2/L3/DRAM) and a
**reuse-distance census** of every folded weight row — combine into a capacity
model with **no fitted parameter** that predicts the hardware:

| | model | measured |
|---|---:|---:|
| L1 hit on weight-row lines | 18.5% | **19.1%** |
| P(L2 hit \| L1 miss) | 70.1% | **69.0%** |

**The cache is already extracting essentially all the locality the access stream
contains.** That explains the whole nine-route catalogue *at once*: **you cannot
prefetch, pack or re-order away a CAPACITY miss.** Nine dead prefetch arms
attacked L1 arrival, while **85.3% of L3 fills and 69.6% of DRAM fills are weight
rows** — the money was one level down.

**This is what a closed question should look like:** not "we tried and failed",
but a mechanism that predicts the measurements and says why every route failed.

### And it hands the ARCHITECTURE a number it did not have

The model leaves exactly one free variable — **bytes per row** — and says width is
**convex**: **1280 → 1024 is a 20% byte cut but a 30% cut in L2-MISSING bytes.**
The standing width finding (768–1536 is ~Elo-neutral at equal time) was measured
on *speed and fit*; this is the first cache-side argument in that decision, and it
points **down**. Relevant to any gen-2 architecture choice after D49.

### Corrections to my brief, all checked

- **`cycles:u` non-robustness confirmed harder than I stated** — its
  byte-identical null moved **further than the arm**. No cycles or wall-clock
  number is quoted anywhere in the study.
- **Branch mispredicts are ~11%, not the 9.4% I gave** — but **study 27 already
  attacked that block and got a NULL on branch misses**, which is why it did not
  go there. My "second largest block" fallback was already closed.
- **"55.05% of miss latency" is IBS *unoverlapped* latency**, and study 51 §9
  warns that is not cycles. I passed it on as though it were.
- **New trap: `.text` md5 is NOT a valid identity check across a Cargo
  feature-set change** — a Rust symbol name embeds the crate metadata hash, which
  appears in objdump's branch annotations. It remains correct for same-feature
  edits. **I have been recommending `.text` md5 as the provenance check all day**,
  including in `engines/canonical/MANIFEST.md`; that guidance needs the caveat.

**Negatives with numbers**, including its own hypothesis **refuted before an arm
was written** (wider tile → L2 streamer trains: L3 fills go *up* +24.3% at
REGS=20), and an IBS latency reading **retracted before publication**.

**Still open, best-priced:** 31.60% of threat folds remain generic, and the win
per converted fold **grows** with the shape (−115 i/fold one-sided vs −322
three-row) — so continue the table, or fix the generic kernel's loop control,
which now looks cheaper.

## Tick — 2026-07-31 16:5x, corrected my own provenance guidance; study 62 commissioned

**Fixed the `.text` md5 requirement I wrote into `engines/canonical/MANIFEST.md`
two ticks ago.** Study 61 showed it is **not a valid identity check across a Cargo
feature-set change** — a Rust symbol name embeds the crate metadata hash, which
surfaces inside `objdump`'s branch annotations, so two semantically identical
builds differ and the check fires falsely. It **remains correct for same-feature
edits**, which is what it is actually for. The manifest now carries the caveat and
points at `tools/perf61/ident61.sh` (node total + wl50 eval hash — quantities that
carry no build metadata) for cross-configuration identity.

Worth noting the sequence: I introduced that requirement to close a provenance
gap, and it was **wrong within two ticks** — corrected in the same file that
carried it, rather than left to be discovered by whoever next promotes a binary.

**Study 62 commissioned on study 61's own best-priced open item:** 31.60% of
threat folds are still generic, and the win per converted fold **grows** with the
shape (−115 i/fold one-sided vs −322 three-row). Two routes — continue the shape
table, or fix the generic kernel's loop control — and the brief tells it to
**price both with `foldcount::THR_PAIR` before building either**, since the shape
of the remaining tail decides which wins and is measurable first. Study 61 judged
the loop-control route cheaper; the brief says verify that judgement rather than

## Tick — 2026-07-31 17:32, where the remaining speed actually is: a PRE-REGISTERED width prediction

Ten studies in, the code-side picture is closed enough to say where the next
speed is **not**:

- **Instruction count** — the marginal conversion to wall-clock is
  **indistinguishable from zero** (studies 57+58 added ~155 shipped i/n and moved
  wall-clock not at all).
- **Memory locality** — closed by study 61's capacity model with **no fitted
  parameter** (L1 hit 18.5% model vs 19.1% measured; P(L2 hit | L1 miss) 70.1% vs
  69.0%). **The cache already extracts essentially all the locality the access
  stream contains**; ten routes closed by number.
- **Branch mispredicts** — study 27's null.

**Study 61's model leaves exactly one free variable: bytes per weight row.** And
it measured that variable to be **convex** — **1280 → 1024 is a 20% byte cut but
a 30% cut in L2-MISSING bytes.** So the biggest remaining speed lever is not in
the code at all; **it is the net architecture.**

### The arithmetic, with a consistency check first

Using the project's own constants — **+44 Elo of fit per width doubling**,
**−45 Elo of speed per width doubling**:

| | net Elo, 1280 → 1024 |
|---|---|
| speed scaling **linear** in bytes | **+0.3** |
| speed scaling with study 61's **convexity** (×1.50) | **+7.6** |

**The linear case lands at ≈0, reproducing the standing finding that width is
Elo-neutral across 768–1536.** That is the check that these are the right
constants — the convexity result is then a change *to a model that already
reproduces the known answer*, not a free-floating estimate.

### Why this is well-motivated rather than wishful

Today's central negative result is that **instruction cuts stopped converting to
time because the engine is memory-bound** (IPC 1.595, ≥47% stall). A width
reduction is not an instruction cut — **it attacks L2-missing bytes, which is the
binding constraint.** It should convert *better* than everything the perf
programme has landed, for precisely the reason those landings converted badly.

### Stated as a prediction, not a recommendation

**This is DERIVED and it is not a proposal to retrain at 1024.** That is a large
commitment and an owner's call. What it is:

- **Falsifiable**: a 1024-wide gen-2 net at equal time should beat a 1280 one by
  roughly **+7.6 Elo**, where the standing model predicts **0**. Those are
  distinguishable outcomes.
- **Carrying three named assumptions**: the 44/45 constants have their own error
  bars; the convexity factor assumes L2-missing bytes map ~linearly to time; and
  the whole thing routes through the ~120 Elo/doubling speed constant.
- **Pre-registered now, before any gen-2 net exists** — so it cannot be fitted
  after the fact. Recorded here for whoever makes the gen-2 architecture call.

**D49:** sb1137, 193k pos/s, four services active. **sb1400 in ~9.5 h.**
**Study 62** working.

## Tick — 2026-07-31 17:46, tried to test the width prediction's SPEED half today. BLOCKED — reported as a null.

The width prediction has two halves: a **fit** half that needs a training run, and
a **speed** half that does not. I went after the speed half, because
`arch-d48w` (1536) and `arch-d48x` (1280) differ **only in width** — CLAUDE.md
records "width 1536 → 1280, **recipe untouched**" — which is exactly the clean
one-variable comparison the convexity claim needs.

**Built `arch-d48w` and it runs. There is no D48W-compatible 1536 net on disk.**
Every 1536 candidate fails the same way:

```
ERROR EvalFile: CENN l2_width 32 != 16 (architecture mismatch)
```

`h1536-smoke-uho-3sb`, `h1536-smoke-s2-farseer-3sb`, `synth_1536` — all three are
from an **older architecture generation** (L2 width 32, not merely a different
accumulator width), and a sweep of every >100 MB `.cenn` on disk found **none**
that loads in a D48W build.

**So the experiment is blocked by artifact availability, not by the idea.** The
1280 arm measured fine (28,809,256,906 instructions, 2,688,395,771 L1d misses,
5,001,562 nodes); there is simply nothing valid to compare it against.

**Reported rather than quietly dropped**, and the partial 1536 reading —
308 M instructions, no node count — is **not** a data point: it is the signature
of a run that aborted at net load. Quoting it as "1536 is faster" would have been
the exact substitution this log keeps catching.

**What it would take:** a 1536-wide net at the current architecture (L2 width 16),
which means a training run — so the speed half is **not** separable from the fit
half with what exists today. **The prediction stays pre-registered and untested,
and that is now a measured fact about our artifacts rather than an assumption.**

**Cleaned up:** `target-w` removed.

**D49:** sb1144, 193k pos/s, four services active. **sb1400 in ~9.2 h.**
**Study 62** working.

number was marginal (p=0.0807).

**The mechanism is understood, not just observed.** Studies 52–58 were dominated
by instruction-count and startup work — half their measured win was process
startup a real game never sees. Studies 61–62 are **fold-kernel dispatch**: work
per node, in the kernels that actually run, which is why only 11.2% is startup.
The memory-bound finding redirected the programme toward the binding constraint
and the value followed.

## Tick — 2026-07-31, study 63 commissioned: settle the `.text` tradeoff by MEASUREMENT, not by threshold

Study 62 left three rungs unlanded — `shape62d` (−3.91 i/n), `shape62e` (−5.37),
`dual62b` (−13.71) — all switchable on `main`, all dropped by its **pre-registered
≥2.0 i/n-per-KB rule**, and all subsequently argued affordable by its own later
panel (`de_no_dispatch_per_slot`: engine **backend-bound 2:1**, so the L1i cost
does not convert into dispatch starvation). **Study 62 refused to act on that**,
correctly, because relaxing a pre-registered rule after seeing the data is the
failure the rule exists to prevent.

**Study 63 is explicitly forbidden from re-arguing that threshold.** Instead:

**A per-KB rule was always a PROXY for "does the code growth cost more than the
instructions save". That question can now be measured directly** — which sidesteps
the rule entirely and is a strictly better instrument than any threshold.

**The box is quiet right now**, which is what makes this possible and has not been
true most of the week. On a quiet box today `wallclock_panel.py` produced its
cleanest result ever — byte-identical null **−0.011% at an exact 20/40 split,
p=1.0000** — where under load the same tool has **refused outright**, with the
null moving further than the arm.

So the brief asks for **a lattice over `{shape62d+shape62e, dual62b}` panelled on
wall-clock at 40 rounds, with `--startup-frame` subtracted using absolute
timings** (the two panel percentages have different baselines and cannot be
subtracted directly), and requires **checking the box is still quiet before and
after each panel — a panel that loses quiet is void, not salvaged.**

**And it names the acceptable null:** if wall-clock genuinely cannot resolve
−3.91 i/n, say so and land nothing there. **A rung too small to measure is a rung
not worth the icache.**

**Secondary target: continue the vein that is paying.** Studies 61–62 delivered
**+3.14 game-relevant Elo from two studies** against **+0.79 from the seven
before**, by attacking fold-kernel dispatch rather than instruction count or
startup. Study 62's biggest win came from a kernel with **no shape dispatch at
all**, which study 61 had screened out on refresh *rate* and row *locality* —
both true, neither about loop control. **Census the remaining fold/refresh sites

**Closed in-brief:** memory locality (no eleventh prefetch arm), the generic

## Tick — 2026-07-31 18:47, DELIBERATELY IDLE: study 63 needs the quiet box

**No work run this tick, on purpose.** Study 63's decisive measurement is a
wall-clock lattice that is only valid on a quiet box, and its brief requires it to
**void any panel that loses quiet rather than salvage it.**

**The standing directive includes "periodically eval the network with a few games
and stats", and I am not doing that this tick.** A screen puts ten `fastchess`
processes on the box and would void whatever panel study 63 is running.

The trade is lopsided:

- **Cost of running the eval:** a sixth mid-cycling point. The five existing
  points (sb620/640/700/900/1060) already pool to **−10.52 ± 8.05**, at **0.65σ
  from sb700** — the conclusion has been stable across 3000 mid-cycling games and
  another 1500 will not change it. And mid-cycling nets sample arbitrary phases of
  a sawtooth whose peaks measurably worsen the fit, so the point is *known* to be
  uninformative by construction.

## Tick — 2026-07-31 19:00, USER RULING: cycling STOPPED, gentle decay from sb1170

**"I think I have had enough of the cyclic LR — let's move to a regular and gentle
cosine decay. Start at the midpoint LR for our cycle and let it go down 0.996 each
sb."**

Two things needed pinning before restarting, and both were put to the owner
because they change the run materially:

1. **Which "midpoint".** At sb1176 the cycle spans **7.064e-5 … 3.532e-6** (peaks
   have decayed 0.985²³). Arithmetic mean is 3.708e-5, geometric is 1.579e-5 — a
   **2.35× difference**. **Ruling: geometric, 1.579e-5.** The arithmetic mean sits
   at 53% of peak, i.e. barely a reduction from where cycling already ran; the
   geometric mean is the true middle of a 20× range at 22% of peak.
2. **Whether to keep the pre-registered sb1400 anneal-to-zero.** **Ruling: no —
   pure 0.996/sb all the way to sb1700**, one rule and no discontinuity. The
   anneal belonged to the cycling plan being replaced.

**Naming note, recorded rather than silently reinterpreted:** the instruction says
"cosine" but specifies **0.996 each sb**, which is *exponential*. I implemented
the number, not the label — the schedule is a geometric decay.

### The schedule

`tools/nnue/runs/d49z_decay_1280.json`: **134 `lr_ramp` points from sb1170 to
sb1700**, `lr = 1.579e-5 × 0.996^(sb−1170)`.

| | |
|---|---|
| start (sb1170) | **1.5790e-05** |
| end (sb1700) | **1.8873e-06** |
| end/start | **0.1195** — matches 0.996⁵³⁰ = 0.1195 exactly |

Points every 4 sb because `lr_fn` uses **`jnp.interp` — piecewise-linear on the
absolute superbatch, clamped flat outside the breakpoints** (`train.py:67`).
Over 4 sb the decay is 1.6%, so linear interpolation tracks the exponential to
<0.02%. Verified against the closed form rather than assumed.

### Restart, done in an order that cannot race

**Watchdog stopped FIRST**, then the trainer — otherwise the watchdog would have
restarted `d49y` on the *old* config within its poll interval. Then: resume point
discovered (**sb1170**, newest sb with both `raw` and `ema-raw`), config written,
launcher repointed (**verified 0 stale references**), trainer relaunched, watchdog
relaunched.

**Running and confirmed advancing: sb1177 and climbing, 193.5k pos/s, GPU 96%.**
All four services active.

**One real cost of the restart, stated:** the log reads `RESUME from sb1170
(fresh Adam transient)` — **resuming discards the optimizer moments.** That is
inherent to any restart, not to this change, but it means the first few
superbatches re-accumulate Adam state.

**Early sanity check:** train-loss sd is **0.000070 over the 470 cycling
superbatches** vs **0.000056 over the first 7 decaying ones** — consistent with
the sawtooth being gone, though n=7 is far too few to claim it. Re-check next tick.

**Consequence for the endpoint plan:** sb1400 is **no longer a schedule boundary**
— it was the cycling plan's anneal jump. The run is now a single smooth leg to
sb1700, so **sb1700 is the readable point**, ~19 h out at 2.16 min/sb.

## Tick — 2026-07-31 19:15, ASIDE (user request): gai-chess vs ARCHON

Built the user's other engine (`/home/nick/projects/gai-chess`) and installed it
at `engines/bin/gai-chess`. 200 games each at fixed 25k nodes, UHO book.

| opponent | result | Elo | Ptnml |
|---|---|---|---|
| ARCHON **PSQT** (no net) | **0–200–0** | −inf | [100, 0, 0, 0, 0] |
| ARCHON **canonical D48X** | **0–199–1** | **−1040.39** | [99, 1, 0, 0, 0] |

It scored its only half-point **against the stronger opponent** — noise at this
scale, but it happened.

**Zero draws in 200 games is a red flag, so I checked the setup rather than
accepting the score.** Both engines honour `go nodes 25000` exactly. The gap is
real and it is **search depth per node**:

| at 25,000 nodes | depth |
|---|---|
| gai-chess (tiny-nnue) | **3** (seldepth 9) |
| ARCHON (D48X) | **12** (seldepth 18) |

The PGN depth histogram over all 8,000 annotated moves is **perfectly bimodal with
an empty gap**: gai-chess occupies depths 1–5 (mode 2), ARCHON occupies 10–15
(mode 13), and **not one move was searched at depth 6–9.** At an identical node
budget the two engines live in disjoint depth ranges — that is pruning
(LMR/null-move/futility), not evaluation.

**A methodological note worth keeping**, because it nearly produced a false bug
report: my first smoke test returned `bestmove 0000` at depth 0 for every `go`
form, which looked like a broken engine. It was **my test harness**. `engine.rs`
runs an async stdin thread that sets the stop flag on `quit`; piping the whole
script at once means `quit` is consumed *before* `go` is processed, so the search
aborts instantly. A real GUI sends `go` and waits for `bestmove`. **Feeding a UCI
engine a here-doc is not the same as playing it.**

**Scale, for context:** gai-chess is **6,078 lines** with a **197 KB** net;
ARCHON is **61,304 lines** with a **138 MB** net — ~700× the net, ~10× the code.
Median game length was 44–50 plies, so it is not collapsing in the opening; it
gets out-searched and ground down.

Box note: these matches were run while study 63 was working, which may have voided
a wall-clock panel. Its brief already requires voiding rather than salvaging any
panel that loses quiet, so the cost is a re-run, not a bad number.

## Tick — 2026-07-31 19:33, RESTART MACHINERY MOVED OFF tmpfs (it was the run's single point of failure)

**The entire restart machinery was living on tmpfs** — launcher, watchdog,
checkpoint reaper and OOM guard all under
`/tmp/claude-.../scratchpad`, with the running `d49wd` unit's `ExecStart`
pointing straight at it.

**That is the one failure the watchdog cannot survive**, and the run has ~19 h
left. If `/tmp` is cleared the watchdog process dies *and* the script it would
restart the trainer with disappears — so a trainer crash after that point would
simply never be recovered. **`/tmp` is RAM-backed on this box** and has already
caused trouble once this project (a full tmpfs made Bash return a bare exit 1 and
killed jobs).

**Relocated to `/home/nick/.local/share/archon-d49` (ext4).** Only the watchdog
needed editing — its `S=` pointed at the scratchpad for the launcher path; the
other three already used durable `/home/nick/...` paths. All four syntax-checked,
then the three watchers were stopped and relaunched from the durable copies.

**The trainer was never touched** — `d49y` still shows `ActiveEnterTimestamp
18:59:44`, the same instance, now at **sb1184** on the decay leg.

**Restart chain re-verified end to end from the new location**, rather than
assumed from the copy succeeding:

| step | result |
|---|---|
| watchdog will exec `$S/d49y_launch.sh`, `S=/home/nick/.local/share/archon-d49` | ✓ |
| resolves to a file that | **EXISTS** |
| which loads | **`runs/d49z_decay_1280.json`** — the decay config, not the old cyclical one |
| survives tmpfs loss | **YES** (ext4) |

That third line mattered: the durable copy had to be taken *after* the schedule
switch, or the watchdog would have silently restarted the run on the **cyclical**
config — reintroducing the schedule the owner just retired.

I have been writing "check the box is quiet before and after each panel, and void
anything that loses it" into briefs by hand. **Put it in the tool.**

`tools/wallclock_panel.py` now samples system-wide non-idle CPU (`/proc/stat`,
1 s window) **before the first round and after the last** — sampled while it is
*not* benching, so anything it sees is somebody else's work — prints both, and
fires a loud **VOID-AND-RERUN** warning above `QUIET_THRESHOLD = 0.15`.

**Smoke-tested live, and it produced its own receipt.** Run against the real PGO
pair while study 63 was panelling:

| same binary pair | reading |
|---|---|
| quiet box, 40 rounds | **−1.879%** |
| loaded box (16.2% → 17.7% busy), 6 rounds | **−4.951%** |

**The loaded reading is 2.6× the true value, and it LICENSED** — the
byte-identical null looked perfectly clean at **+0.081%**.

**That is the point, and it is worth stating precisely:** interleaving the null
within each round makes a panel robust to *steady* load, which is why the null
stayed clean. It does **not** protect against load that inflates both arms
multiplicatively — the null and the arm both slow down, the null still cancels,
and the *ratio* is wrong anyway. **The null cannot detect this class of error.
The load check can.**

This closes a gap that has been live all session: several panels were run under
load, and the only defence was me remembering to look at `ps`.

**D49 sb1204, 34 superbatches into the decay leg.** Heldout EMA falling

**The decay leg is already below the cycling leg's best, after 30 superbatches.**

### Three reasons that is weaker evidence than it appears

1. **It does not isolate the schedule.** The decay leg runs *after* the cycling
   leg, so it is simply **later in training** and should be better regardless of
   what schedule it uses. **This is not evidence the decay is better than
   continued cycling would have been** — that comparison would need a
   counterfactual we did not run.
2. **n = 3 heldout points.** The session's own lesson from this week is that a
   claim defensible at n≈4 expires as n grows; the inverse applies here — a
   pattern at n=3 is not yet a claim.
3. **Loss is not Elo.** The standing receipt is **44e-6 of heldout alongside ~30
   Elo of games**. A 43e-6 improvement converts to nothing in particular.

**What it IS good for:** the abort condition is comfortably not firing, and
training is still making progress at sb1204 rather than sitting in place. That is
the whole job of this instrument.

**One genuine asymmetry worth noting in the comparison's favour:** the cycling
leg's "best" was itself a **favourable-phase sample** — its EMA was contaminated
by a sawtooth whose troughs read low, so 0.010607 is cycling's *best case*. The
decay leg has no phase to sample, so its 0.010564 is an ordinary reading. The
comparison is therefore conservative rather than flattering.

**Phase-lock test still not run:** n=34 new-leg superbatches, needs ~40.

### The null is powered, which is what makes it mean anything

A non-significant result with n=6 per bin invites "underpowered". It is not:

| | |
|---|---|
| decay-leg standard error | 0.000023 |
| detectable at 2σ | 0.000046 |
| cycling effect size | **0.000116** |
| **cycling effect ÷ detection threshold** | **2.5×** |

**The test can comfortably see a cycling-magnitude sawtooth, and does not see
one.** That converts −1.32σ from "no evidence" into "evidence of no effect at the
relevant scale."

**Two independent confirmations now agree**, which is the standard this log has
been holding itself to:

1. **Structural** — the ramp is monotone decreasing across all 134 points, checked

2. **Empirical** — the phase-lock statistic that read **+11.84σ** under cycling

## Tick — 2026-07-31 20:46, study 63 found TWO DEFECTS IN MY PANEL TOOL — and I had been blaming the box

Study 63 is mid-panel and has already committed a fix to `tools/wallclock_panel.py`
titled *"the wall-clock arbiter was comparing a tmpfs binary against two NVMe
ones."* Its evidence is a **400-round** PGO panel where the byte-identical null
read **+0.646% at p < 1e-4** — in **every rotation phase and every quarter**, with
the three position medians inside 0.25% of each other. **Not position, not drift.
Systematic instrument bias.**

| defect | mechanism |
|---|---|
| **1. null copy on tmpfs** | `tempfile.mkdtemp()` lands in `/tmp` (tmpfs here) while base and arm sit on NVMe. Executing an ELF out of tmpfs ≠ out of the ext4 page cache. Copy now lands beside the base binary. |
| **2. runs not pinned** | every counter panel in the 45–62 chain pins to one physical core; the wall-clock panel did not. Per-round sd **3.58% unpinned vs 0.98–1.13% pinned**. `--cpu` now defaults to core 2. |

**Both were mine.** I wrote that tool this afternoon and smoke-tested it — and the
smoke test passed, because a *broken* instrument that refuses is
indistinguishable from a *working* instrument on a noisy box.

### What this does to the numbers I published — worked through, not waved at

**Defect 1 biases the NULL only, not the arm.** The null compares
copy(tmpfs) against base(NVMe) — asymmetric. The arm compares arm(NVMe) against
base(NVMe) — **symmetric, so unbiased**. **Defect 2 is variance, not bias**: it
widens both bars and moves neither centre.

**So the +3.14 game-relevant Elo for studies 61–62 stands on its centre**
(arm −1.879%, 29/40, p=0.0064), measured with an instrument noisier than it needed
to be. That is the honest position, and it is *not* a get-out — it happens to be
what the mechanism implies, and I would be recording a retraction here if the
asymmetry had touched the arm.

### The sharper consequence, which does land on me

**A biased-high null makes the licence `|null| < |arm|/2` HARDER to pass. The tool
has been issuing FALSE REFUSALS.** Study 62's panel that "refused twice, with the
byte-identical null moving further than the arm" — which I recorded as *"the
difference a quiet box makes"* — was plausibly **my tool's own asymmetry, not the
box at all.**

**And I had been building on that misreading.** I wrote a load-detector this very
afternoon on the premise that null failures meant contention, and told study 63's
brief that panels refusing under load was "correct behaviour, not a bug." Part of
it was a bug — in the harness, put there by me.

**Study 63's own lesson is the one to keep:** *"the null is not a formality, it is
the only part of the harness that can fail loudly, and when it fails the answer is
to FIX THE INSTRUMENT, not to re-run hoping for a quieter box."* I did the second
thing repeatedly today.

The fix is on its branch and will merge with its report; I am not cherry-picking
it mid-study.

**D49:** sb1218, heldout ema **0.010539**, 187k pos/s, four services active.

## Tick — 2026-07-31 21:01, the null-attribution lesson saved to memory

Recorded the panel-tool finding as a standing lesson, because it is a *habit*
error rather than a one-off bug and it will recur otherwise:

> **When a byte-identical null misbehaves, check the harness for asymmetry
> between the arms BEFORE touching the environment** — different filesystem,
> path, scheduling, order, cache state. Only once the harness is symmetric is
> "the box was busy" a permitted explanation.

**The receipt is that I got this backwards all day**, and it cost more than a
number: a biased-high null made the licence harder to pass, the tool issued false
refusals, I recorded one as *"the difference a quiet box makes"*, then wrote a
load-detector on that premise and told study 63's brief that refusing under load
was "correct behaviour, not a bug."

Also recorded: **why the smoke test missed it.** A *broken* instrument that
refuses is indistinguishable from a *working* instrument on a noisy box — both
print `NOT LICENSED`. That is why "I tested it and it refused sensibly" was not
evidence of correctness.

And the discipline that kept the damage contained: **work out which half a defect
touches before retracting or defending.** The tmpfs asymmetry is null-only (null
is copy-vs-base across filesystems; the arm is NVMe-vs-NVMe), and unpinned runs
are variance not bias — so the arm centres survived. **That was the mechanism's
verdict, not a convenience**, and a retraction would have been recorded had it
gone the other way.

**D49:** sb1225, heldout ema **0.010522** — still falling
(0.010564 → 0.010539 → 0.010522), 188k pos/s, four services active, 146 GB disk.
**Study 63** still working; its tool fix merges with its report rather than being
cherry-picked mid-study.

## Tick — 2026-07-31 21:4x, RETRACTION: two published Elo figures measured NOTHING

Study 63 merged (gate green: `All checks passed.`, `Bench: 587683`). Chasing a
0.021s round time in its repaired tool, I found a defect worse than the two it
found — **and it is mine.**

**`engines/canonical/net.cenn` is a symlink into gitignored `data/`, so it dangles
in every fresh worktree.** `wallclock_panel.py`'s default `--net` resolves relative
to the **script's own location**, i.e. to whichever worktree it runs from. With a
dangling net the engine prints `reading NNUE file: No such file` and **exits
having searched ZERO nodes, in ~0.02 s.** The panel then reports a median over
three near-instant exits — **a plausible-looking percentage with nothing behind
it.**

This is **perf study 60's trap, in a second tool.** Study 60 found it in
`build_pgo.sh` and added a guard. I wrote this tool afterwards and did not apply
the lesson.

### What is retracted, and what survives

| figure | how it was run | verdict |
|---|---|---|
| **+1.77 Elo** (studies 52–56) | standalone `wc40.py`, ROOT hardcoded to the **main checkout**, `os.path.realpath` | **STANDS** |
| **+1.58 Elo** (studies 52–58) | `wallclock_panel.py` from `mainwt`, **no `--net`** | **VOID** |
| **+3.14 Elo** (studies 61–62) | `wallclock_panel.py` from `mainwt`, **no `--net`** | **VOID** |

**And the redirect receipt goes with them.** "Two studies after the redirect
delivered ~4× the game-relevant Elo of the seven before" compared **+0.79
(derived from the void 52–58 panel)** against **+3.14 (void)**. **Both sides were
measurements of an engine that never searched.** That claim is withdrawn in full —
not weakened, withdrawn.

**What still stands independently of any panel:** the instruction counts
(`instructions:u`, 0.000 null, load-robust, verified by my own rebuilds at each
merge), the gates, node-identity, and study 63's own kill of the three rungs —
which it measured with an explicit net on a repaired instrument.

### Fixed in the tool, not just noted

Two guards, matching study 60's shape: **the net must be a readable file**, and
**a probe run must print `Nodes searched`** before any round is timed. Verified: it
now refuses the dangling worktree default (printing the resolved path it tried)
and proceeds on an explicit resolved net.

Also added, from study 63's finding that a before/after census cannot see a job
that starts *and* ends between the checks: a **mid-run stability check** on the
base binary's own per-round timings, flagging >2% first-half/second-half drift as
VOID.

### The pattern worth naming

Three tools have now had the same dangling-symlink defect — `build_pgo.sh`,
`wallclock_panel.py`, and (as a near-miss) `screen.sh`, which I had already given
a load guard for exactly this reason. **A default that resolves relative to the
running script is a trap in a repo that uses worktrees**, and the symptom is never
an error — it is a fast, clean-looking number.

## Tick — 2026-07-31 23:0x, SPEED, MEASURED FOUR WAYS (user request)

Built the **study-20-era commit `762d9860` (2026-07-29) with PGO** and raced it
against current main and two Stockfish builds — 13 positions (startpos + UHO
book), 2 s each, 1 thread, 16 MB, **pinned to core 2, all four interleaved**:

| engine | median nps | depth | vs old ARCHON |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| ARCHON old (study-20 era) | 712,335 | 22.0 | 1.000× |
| **ARCHON new** (post-study-63) | **792,992** | 23.0 | **1.113×** |
| **SF dev `eca43a97`** (SINGLE-net, PGO) | **1,001,610** | 24.0 | **1.406×** |
| Stockfish 18 (dual-net, stock) | 901,469 | 22.0 | 1.266× |

**The whole perf programme since study 20 is worth ~11% of real speed** —
measured directly, not derived. **Single-net SF is 1.26× our current speed.**

### Two corrections the measurement forced

1. **The 1.6M nps figure was an artifact of the position set.** `wl50.fens` has a
   **median of 12 pieces** (21 of 50 at ≤11) — it is endgame-heavy, the fastest
   case for NNUE. Same binary: **2.74M nps** on a 5-piece endgame,
   **~0.8–1.0M** on real game positions. A 3× spread from composition alone.
2. **The owner caught a net-configuration error before it reached a conclusion.**
   `stockfish18` (release) is **dual-net** (`EvalFile` + `EvalFileSmall`); the
   pinned dev build `eca43a97` is **single-net**, like ours. And the single-net
   build is **faster** (1.41× vs 1.27×), so comparing against SF18 would have
   flattered us by ~11 points. The apples-to-apples number is **1.26×**.

**Absolute values are load-dependent** — the same binary read 908k an hour earlier
and 793k here — but **the ratios are what interleaving protects**, and they held
across two independent runs (old→new 1.103× then 1.113×). Depth is only roughly
comparable across engines, since depth means different things under different
pruning.

**This is the cleanest speed number of the session**, and notably it did **not**
come from the wall-clock panel — it came from a direct interleaved race between
real binaries. Given the panel has had four defects found in it today, that is
worth noting as a methodological point: **racing two binaries on real positions is
harder to get wrong than differencing a synthetic frame.**

**Consistency check:** ~11% of real speed against **−669 i/n (−10.6%)** of
instructions. Those are not the same thing and should not be equated, but they are
the same order — and the shortfall is the same story study 63 told: late-stage
instruction cuts come off the **tail** of a backend-bound-2:1 machine.

**D49:** sb1252, heldout ema **0.010492** — still the run's lowest, still falling.
